John cross-references

875 completed source verses; 886 retained pairs; 878 source units read; 3 deferred units.

First unfinished: John 2:2. Next new source review in this book: none. All original source units in this book have been read. Overall next new source: Galatians 1:1.

Completed means an initial curated cross-reference pass: the complete supplied English source unit and each retained target passage were read, and each pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean exhaustive coverage or independent specialist approval.

John 1:1

In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.

  1. Genesis 1:1–1:3 allusion

    John's beginning, divine Word, creation context and light recall Genesis' beginning with God making heaven and earth, speaking, and bringing light. This is a creation-pattern comparison, not a claim that Genesis explicitly states John's full account of the Word being with God and being God.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 1:1 In the beginning God made heaven and earth.

    Genesis 1:2 Now the earth was unseen and unformed, and darkness was over the deep; and the Spirit of God was moving over the water.

    Genesis 1:3 And God said, “Let there be light.” And there was light.

    Pinned target record

John 1:2

He was in the beginning with God.

  1. 1 John 1:1–1:2 teaching parallel

    The Word's being with God in the beginning is compared with the eternal life that was with the Father and was manifested to the witnesses. Both passages join prior divine fellowship with subsequent revelation, while Word, life, God and Father retain the actual expressions used in their respective contexts.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 1:1 What was from the beginning, what we have heard, what we have seen with our eyes, what we looked upon and our hands touched, concerning the word of life—

    1 John 1:2 and the life was manifested, and we have seen and bear witness and announce to you the eternal life that was with the Father and was manifested to us—

    Pinned target record

John 1:3

All things came into being through Him, and without Him not one thing came into being. What has come into being

  1. Colossians 1:16–1:17 teaching parallel

    John says all things came into being through Him and excludes any exception; Colossians says all things, heavenly and earthly, were created through and for Him and that He is before all things. The comparison concerns Christ's comprehensive relation to creation, without changing John's sentence division where the final clause continues into 1:4.

    Read matching passage

    Colossians 1:16 because in Him all things were created, in the heavens and upon the earth, visible and invisible, whether thrones or dominions or rulers or authorities. All things have been created through Him and for Him.

    Colossians 1:17 And He is before all things, and in Him all things hold together.

    Pinned target record

John 1:4

in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.

  1. John 8:12 teaching parallel

    The prologue joins life in Him with humanity's light; Jesus later identifies Himself as the world's Light and promises the follower the light of life. This is an explicit recurring life-and-light connection, while the earlier syntax remains continuous with the end of 1:3.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:12 Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

    Pinned target record

John 1:5

And the light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not overcome it.

  1. John 12:35–12:36 teaching parallel

    The light shines in darkness that did not overcome it; the later saying urges people to walk and believe while the Light is present so darkness may not overtake them. The contrast preserves unconquered light versus a warning about people being overtaken, rather than transferring the first statement into an unconditional guarantee about every person.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:35 Then Jesus said to them, “For a little time longer the Light is among you. Walk while you have the Light, so that darkness may not overtake you. The one walking in the darkness does not know where he is going.

    John 12:36 While you have the Light, believe in the Light, so that you may become sons of light.” Jesus spoke these things, and went away and hid Himself from them.

    Pinned target record

John 1:6

There came a man sent from God; his name was John.

  1. Luke 3:2–3:3 narrative parallel

    John is introduced as a man sent from God; Luke names John son of Zechariah as the recipient of God's word in the wilderness and describes his ensuing baptismal proclamation. Both establish the Baptist's divine commission, with Luke supplying its particular setting and activity rather than a duplicate of John's prologue.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 3:2 during the high priesthood of Annas and Caiaphas, God’s word came to John, Zechariah’s son, in the wilderness.

    Luke 3:3 He went into all the region around the Jordan, proclaiming a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins,

    Pinned target record

John 1:7

He came as a witness, to bear witness concerning the Light, so that all might believe through him.

  1. John 5:33–5:35 teaching parallel

    The prologue says John's witness serves others' belief; Jesus later recalls John's testimony to truth and explains that He mentions it so His hearers may be saved. Both passages describe John's testimony as directed toward others' response, and the later passage calls him a shining lamp rather than making him the ultimate Light.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:33 You have sent to John, and he has testified to the truth.

    John 5:34 But I do not receive testimony from a person; rather, I say these things so that you may be saved.

    John 5:35 He was the lamp that burned and shone, and you were willing to rejoice for an hour in his light.

    Pinned target record

John 1:8

He was not the Light, but came to bear witness concerning the Light.

  1. John 5:35–5:36 teaching parallel

    John is distinguished from the Light to whom he bears witness; Jesus later calls John a burning, shining lamp but describes a greater testimony in His own Father-given works. The distinction permits the Baptist's figurative light as a lamp without identifying him as the true Light of the prologue.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:35 He was the lamp that burned and shone, and you were willing to rejoice for an hour in his light.

    John 5:36 But I have testimony greater than John’s: for the works the Father has given Me to complete, the very works I do, bear witness concerning Me that the Father has sent Me.

    Pinned target record

John 1:9

The true Light, which gives light to every person, was coming into the world.

  1. John 8:12 teaching parallel

    The prologue announces the true Light coming into the world and illuminating people; Jesus later calls Himself the Light of the world and connects following Him with life rather than darkness. The shared worldwide light language does not silently equate being illuminated with every person's believing response.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:12 Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

    Pinned target record

John 1:10

He was in the world, and the world came into being through Him, and the world did not know Him.

  1. John 3:19 teaching parallel

    The world fails to recognize the One present in it; the later discourse explains judgment in terms of the Light coming into the world while people prefer darkness because their deeds are evil. This compares failed recognition with the later account of adverse response to the same light motif; the creation clause remains additional in the prologue.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:19 And this is the judgment: the Light has come into the world, and people loved the darkness more than the Light, for their deeds were evil.

    Pinned target record

John 1:11

He came to what was His own, and His own did not receive Him.

  1. Luke 4:24–4:30 narrative parallel

    John speaks of failure to receive Him among His own; Luke narrates Jesus' saying about a prophet's unacceptability at home and the resulting hostile hometown response. Luke provides a concrete rejection scene, while John's broader expression His own is not reduced exclusively to Nazareth or made a claim that every member of a people rejects Him.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 4:24 He said, “Amen, I tell you, no prophet is acceptable in his homeland.

    Luke 4:25 But in truth I tell you, there were many widows in Israel in Elijah’s days, when heaven was shut for three years and six months and a great famine came over the whole land.

    Luke 4:26 Yet Elijah was sent to none of them except to a widow at Zarephath in Sidon’s territory.

    Luke 4:27 And there were many leprous people in Israel in the time of Elisha the prophet, but none of them was cleansed except Naaman the Syrian.”

    Luke 4:28 Everyone in the synagogue was filled with anger when they heard these things.

    Luke 4:29 They rose, threw Him out of the city, and led Him to the brow of the hill on which their city was built, intending to hurl Him down.

    Luke 4:30 But He passed through their midst and went on His way.

    Pinned target record

John 1:12

But as many as received Him, He gave them authority to become children of God—to those who believe in His name,

  1. Galatians 3:26 teaching parallel

    John connects receiving Christ and believing in His name with becoming God's children; Galatians calls its hearers God's sons through faith in Christ Jesus. Both relate membership in God's family to faith in Christ, using authority to become children and being sons as their respective expressions.

    Read matching passage

    Galatians 3:26 For you are all sons of God through faith in Christ Jesus.

    Pinned target record

John 1:13

who were born not from bloods, nor from the will of flesh, nor from the will of a man, but from God.

  1. 1 Peter 1:23 teaching parallel

    John contrasts divine birth with bloods and human or fleshly will; Peter describes birth anew from imperishable rather than perishable seed through God's living, enduring word. Both distinguish a God-given birth from ordinary mortal derivation, without replacing John's stated negatives or turning the different birth images into identical wording.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 1:23 having been born anew, not from perishable seed but from imperishable, through the living and enduring word of God.

    Pinned target record

John 1:14

And the Word became flesh and dwelt among us, and we beheld His glory, glory as of the Only-begotten from the Father, full of grace and truth.

  1. Philippians 2:6–2:8 teaching parallel

    John says the Word became flesh and dwelt among witnesses who saw His glory; Philippians describes Christ in God's form taking a slave's form and human likeness, then humbling Himself to death. The comparison concerns divine Christ entering human existence, with John's glory-and-revelation emphasis and Philippians' humility-and-obedience emphasis preserved.

    Read matching passage

    Philippians 2:6 who, existing in the form of God, did not regard being equal with God as something to exploit,

    Philippians 2:7 but emptied Himself, taking the form of a slave, coming to be in the likeness of human beings; and being found in appearance as a human being,

    Philippians 2:8 He humbled Himself, becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross.

    Pinned target record

John 1:15

John bears witness concerning Him and has cried out, saying, “This was He of whom I said, ‘The One coming after me has come before me, because He was before me.’”

  1. John 1:30 narrative parallel

    The Baptist's testimony about the One coming after him who has come before him because He was before him is explicitly repeated when he identifies Jesus. This is an internal recurrence of the priority saying, not another incident independently establishing a different birth order.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:30 This is He of whom I said, ‘After me comes a man who has come before me, because He was before me.’

    Pinned target record

John 1:16

For from His fullness we all received, and grace in place of grace.

  1. Ephesians 4:7 teaching parallel

    John's community receives from Christ's fullness and grace in place of grace; Ephesians says grace was given to each according to Christ's gift. Both describe received grace originating in Christ, while Ephesians' distribution language does not resolve or replace John's particular grace-in-place-of-grace expression.

    Read matching passage

    Ephesians 4:7 But grace was given to each one of us according to the measure of Christ’s gift.

    Pinned target record

John 1:17

For the Law was given through Moses; grace and truth came through Jesus Christ.

  1. Exodus 24:12 thematic parallel

    John identifies Moses as the intermediary of the Law; Exodus records God's promise to give Moses the written stone tablets, law and commandments. This supplies a concrete scriptural basis for Moses' law-receiving role, while the grace-and-truth statement about Jesus is additional in John.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 24:12 And the Lord said to Moses, “Come up to Me into the mountain, and remain there; and I will give you the tablets of stone, the law and the commandments which I have written to give them as law.”

    Pinned target record

  2. Romans 5:20–5:21 teaching parallel

    John places the Law given through Moses beside grace and truth coming through Jesus Christ; Romans places the law's arrival alongside abounding grace reigning toward eternal life through Jesus Christ. This is a specific law-and-grace comparison, without making the two authors' explanations or the phrase grace and truth identical.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 5:20 But law came in alongside, so that the trespass might increase; but where sin increased, grace abounded beyond measure,

    Romans 5:21 so that, just as sin reigned in death, so also grace might reign through righteousness for eternal life through Jesus Christ our Lord.

    Pinned target record

John 1:18

No one has ever seen God; the only-begotten God, who is in the bosom of the Father, He has made Him known.

  1. Matthew 11:27 teaching parallel

    John presents the only-begotten God in the Father's bosom as the One who makes God known; Matthew presents the Son's unique knowledge of the Father and His ability to reveal Him. Both give the Son a distinctive revelatory relation to the Father. John's supplied wording only-begotten God is preserved rather than replaced with another reading.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:19

And this is the testimony of John, when the Jews sent priests and Levites from Jerusalem to him, to ask him, “Who are you?”

  1. John 5:33 narrative parallel

    The priests-and-Levites delegation asks John for his identity; Jesus later explicitly recalls people having sent to John and his testimony to truth. The later retrospective notice is compared with this narrated inquiry, without adding names of envoys or claiming that every detail of the delegation is repeated.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:33 You have sent to John, and he has testified to the truth.

    Pinned target record

John 1:20

And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”

  1. John 3:28 narrative parallel

    John's denial I am not the Christ is explicitly recalled later when he asks his hearers to witness that he had said this and had been sent before Him. This is a deliberate internal repetition of the Baptist's stated role, rather than an independent competing claim to the title.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:28 You yourselves bear witness for me that I said, ‘I am not the Christ, but I have been sent before Him.’

    Pinned target record

John 1:21

They asked him, “What, then? Are you Elijah?” And he said, “I am not.” “Are you the Prophet?” And he answered, “No.”

  1. Luke 1:17 thematic parallel

    John denies being Elijah when questioned; Luke's angel describes his going before the Lord in Elijah's spirit and power. The comparison preserves both the denial and the stated likeness of mission, without rewriting either as a simple assertion that John is the same historical Elijah or denying John's prophetic activity.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:17 He himself will go before Him in Elijah’s spirit and power, to turn fathers’ hearts toward children and the disobedient to the understanding of the righteous, to prepare for the Lord a people made ready.”

    Pinned target record

  2. Deuteronomy 18:15–18:18 allusion

    The envoys' question about the Prophet is compared with Moses' promise that God will raise a prophet like him from the people and put His word in that prophet's mouth. This supplies scriptural background for the expected prophetic figure, not proof of every assumption behind the envoys' question or a denial that John functions as a prophet in other passages.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 18:15 The Lord your God shall raise up unto you a prophet from among your brethren, like unto me; you shall hearken unto him,

    Deuteronomy 18:16 according to all that you asked of the Lord your God in Horeb, in the day of the assembly, saying: We will no more hear the voice of the Lord our God, neither will we see this great fire any more, lest we die.

    Deuteronomy 18:17 And the Lord said unto me: All that they have spoken is right.

    Deuteronomy 18:18 I will raise up for them a prophet from among their brethren, like unto you, and will put My word in his mouth; and he shall speak unto them all that I command him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:22

So they said to him, “Who are you, so that we may give an answer to those who sent us? What do you say about yourself?”

  1. Luke 7:19–7:22 narrative parallel

    Both scenes send a delegation to ask about a minister's identity and obtain a report for the senders. The envoys questioning John the Baptist seek his own account after the proposed titles are denied; in Luke, John the Baptist sends disciples to Jesus and Jesus tells them what to report back. Questioned person, sender, answers and occasions differ, so the parallel is the concrete inquiry-and-report structure rather than one delegation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:19 and sent them to the Lord, saying, “Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?”

    Luke 7:20 When the men came to Him, they said, “John the Baptist sent us to Thee, saying, ‘Art Thou the One who is coming, or should we expect another?’”

    Luke 7:21 At that hour He healed many of diseases, afflictions and evil spirits, and graciously gave sight to many blind people.

    Luke 7:22 He answered them, “Go and report to John what you have seen and heard: the blind recover sight, the lame walk, leprous people are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead are raised, and the poor receive good news.

    Pinned target record

John 1:23

He said, “I am a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord,’ just as Isaiah the prophet said.”

  1. Isaiah 40:3 quotation

    John explicitly identifies himself with Isaiah's wilderness voice and the command to straighten the Lord's way. The actual Isaiah unit speaks of preparing the Lord's way and making straight the paths of our God. The named citation is retained with the differing wording of the supplied passages, rather than silently making their formulations identical.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 40:3 A voice of one crying in the wilderness: Prepare the way of the Lord; make straight the paths of our God.

    Pinned target record

John 1:24

Now those sent were from the Pharisees.

  1. Luke 7:29–7:30 thematic parallel

    The Pharisaic background of John's envoys is compared with Luke's explicit contrast between recipients of John's baptism and Pharisees and Law experts described as rejecting God's purpose and not being baptized by him. Both concern that religious group's relation to this particular baptismal ministry. Luke's stated response is not assigned to these individual envoys or made proof of their identities, motives or personal baptism history.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 7:29 When all the people and the tax collectors heard, they acknowledged God’s righteousness, having been baptized with John’s baptism.

    Luke 7:30 But the Pharisees and experts in the law rejected God’s purpose for themselves, not having been baptized by him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:25

And they questioned him and said to him, “Why, then, do you baptize if you are not the Christ, nor Elijah, nor the Prophet?”

  1. Mark 11:28–11:33 narrative parallel

    The envoys challenge John's authority to baptize in the absence of the titles they propose; Mark's authority dispute asks whether John's baptism came from heaven or humans. Both concern the authorization of John's baptism, while Mark's later question is posed by Jesus to temple authorities and is not the same interrogation of John.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 11:28 and said to Him, “By what authority art Thou doing these things? Or who gave Thee this authority to do them?”

    Mark 11:29 Jesus said to them, “I will ask you one question. Answer Me, and I will tell you by what authority I do these things.

    Mark 11:30 Was John’s baptism from heaven or from humans? Answer Me.”

    Mark 11:31 They reasoned with one another, saying, “If we say, ‘From heaven,’ He will say, ‘Why then did you not believe him?’

    Mark 11:32 But should we say, ‘From humans’?” They feared the crowd, for everyone held that John truly was a prophet.

    Mark 11:33 They answered Jesus, “We do not know.” Jesus said to them, “Neither do I tell you by what authority I do these things.”

    Pinned target record

John 1:26

John answered them, saying, “I baptize in water. Among you stands One whom you do not know,

  1. Mark 1:8 narrative parallel

    John states that he baptizes in water and distinguishes the One standing among his questioners; Mark similarly contrasts his water baptism with the coming One's baptism in the Holy Spirit. John's notice that the hearers do not know that One remains particular to this exchange, with the Spirit identification developed later in 1:33.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:8 I baptized you with water, but He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit.”

    Pinned target record

John 1:27

the One coming after me, whose sandal strap I am not worthy to untie.”

  1. Mark 1:7 narrative parallel

    Both accounts give the Baptist's coming-after-me saying and his unworthiness to untie the stronger One's sandal strap. Mark adds stooping and explicitly says stronger; John places the saying within the delegation's inquiry. The close saying parallel does not combine their narrative order.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:7 He proclaimed, saying, “The One stronger than I is coming after me. I am not worthy to stoop down and untie the strap of His sandals.

    Pinned target record

John 1:28

These things happened in Bethany beyond the Jordan, where John was baptizing.

  1. John 10:40 narrative parallel

    The earlier scene is located beyond the Jordan where John baptizes; the later narrative explicitly returns beyond the Jordan to the place where John was first baptizing. This is a retrospective location link within John, not a settled modern site identification or an equation with the Bethany of Lazarus's household.

    Read matching passage

    John 10:40 And He went away again beyond the Jordan, to the place where John was first baptizing, and remained there.

    Pinned target record

John 1:29

The next day he saw Jesus coming toward him and said, “Behold, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!

  1. 1 Peter 1:18–1:19 thematic parallel

    John identifies Jesus as God's Lamb taking away the world's sin; Peter describes ransom through Christ's precious blood as that of an unblemished, spotless lamb. Both use lamb imagery for Christ's saving work. Peter specifies blood and ransom, while John's world's-sin wording is not narrowed or replaced by those expressions.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 1:18 knowing that you were ransomed from the futile conduct handed down from your ancestors, not with perishable things, silver or gold,

    1 Peter 1:19 but with precious blood, as of a lamb without blemish and without spot—the blood of Christ,

    Pinned target record

John 1:30

This is He of whom I said, ‘After me comes a man who has come before me, because He was before me.’

  1. John 1:15 narrative parallel

    The Baptist's identification of Jesus repeats the earlier coming-after-yet-before-me testimony with the same because-He-was-before-me explanation. This is an explicit internal cross-reference to the witness already announced, not an independently dated second account of their births.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:15 John bears witness concerning Him and has cried out, saying, “This was He of whom I said, ‘The One coming after me has come before me, because He was before me.’”

    Pinned target record

John 1:31

And I did not know Him, but I came baptizing in water for this reason: so that He might be revealed to Israel.”

  1. Luke 1:76–1:77 thematic parallel

    John explains his water-baptizing mission as revealing Jesus to Israel; Zechariah's prophecy assigns his child the role of preceding the Lord and giving His people knowledge of salvation. Both give John's ministry a preparatory, revelatory purpose. Luke does not explain John's personal statement I did not know Him or settle the sequence of recognition in other accounts.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:76 And you also, child, will be called prophet of the Most High, for you will go before the Lord to prepare His ways,

    Luke 1:77 to give His people knowledge of salvation in the forgiveness of their sins,

    Pinned target record

John 1:32

And John testified, saying, “I have beheld the Spirit descending like a dove from heaven, and He remained upon Him.

  1. Mark 1:10 narrative parallel

    Both passages describe the Spirit's dove-like descent at Jesus: Mark narrates His seeing the heavens torn open and the Spirit descending into Him, while John reports the Baptist's testimony that the Spirit descended from heaven and remained upon Him. Observer, preposition and remaining-language retain their actual differences.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:10 Immediately as He came up out of the water, He saw the heavens being torn open and the Spirit descending like a dove into Him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:33

And I did not know Him, but the One who sent me to baptize in water said to me, ‘The One upon whom you see the Spirit descending and remaining—this is the One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit.’

  1. Matthew 3:11 narrative parallel

    John's commissioned sign identifies the One who baptizes in the Holy Spirit, distinct from his own water baptism; Matthew likewise contrasts John's water baptism with the stronger One's baptism in the Holy Spirit and fire. Matthew does not supply John's recognition-sign wording, and its fire clause is not inserted into John.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 3:11 I baptize you in water for repentance, but the One coming after me is stronger than I, whose sandals I am not fit to carry. He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire.

    Pinned target record

John 1:34

And I have seen and have testified that this is the Son of God.”

  1. Matthew 3:17 narrative parallel

    The Baptist testifies that Jesus is God's Son; Matthew records the heavenly voice identifying Jesus as the beloved Son in whom God is pleased. Both concern Jesus' sonship at the baptismal setting, while a human witness and a voice from heaven remain distinct speakers and forms of testimony.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 3:17 And behold, a voice from the heavens said, “This is My beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased.”

    Pinned target record

John 1:35

The next day John was standing there again, and two of his disciples,

  1. John 1:40 narrative parallel

    The later unit explicitly identifies Andrew as one of the two who heard the Baptist and followed Jesus, continuing the pair introduced standing with John here and acting in 1:36–39. This is a specific internal participant clarification. The second disciple remains unnamed, and the later verse does not independently restate the next-day timing or standing posture.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:40 Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:36

and looking at Jesus as He walked, he said, “Behold, the Lamb of God!”

  1. John 1:29 narrative parallel

    The Baptist repeats the designation Lamb of God while looking at Jesus; the preceding day's fuller identification adds the taking away of the world's sin. This is a repeated identifying title within the narrative, with the extra explanatory words retained in 1:29 rather than added to the shorter saying.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:29 The next day he saw Jesus coming toward him and said, “Behold, the Lamb of God, who takes away the sin of the world!

    Pinned target record

John 1:37

The two disciples heard him speaking and followed Jesus.

  1. John 1:40 narrative parallel

    The two hear the Baptist and follow Jesus; the later unit explicitly identifies Andrew as one of those two who heard from John and followed. This is an internal clarification of one participant in the narrated action. It does not name the second disciple or equate the pair with a differently named calling scene.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:40 Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:38

Jesus turned and, seeing them following, said to them, “What do you seek?” They said to Him, “Rabbi”—which, translated, means Teacher—“where art Thou staying?”

  1. John 20:16 thematic parallel

    The disciples address Jesus as Rabbi, which the narrator translates Teacher; Mary later addresses the risen Jesus as Rabbouni, likewise glossed Teacher. The link concerns the expressly translated forms of address. Mary's resurrection encounter does not reproduce the earlier disciples' question about where Jesus is staying.

    Read matching passage

    John 20:16 Jesus says to her, “Mary.” Turning, she says to Him in Hebrew, “Rabbouni!”—which means Teacher.

    Pinned target record

John 1:39

He said to them, “Come, and you will see.” So they came and saw where He was staying, and stayed with Him that day. It was about the tenth hour.

  1. John 1:46 narrative parallel

    Jesus' invitation Come, and you will see is echoed by Philip's invitation Come and see to Nathanael. Both move an inquirer toward personal encounter with Jesus, with Jesus and Philip as different inviters. Philip's later words do not establish the location or tenth-hour timing of the first disciples' stay.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

    Pinned target record

John 1:40

Andrew, Simon Peter’s brother, was one of the two who heard from John and followed Him.

  1. Matthew 4:18–4:20 narrative parallel

    Both passages identify Andrew as Simon Peter's brother and describe his following Jesus. John places Andrew among the Baptist's two hearers; Matthew places the brothers at their fishing work when Jesus calls them. These are related discipleship accounts with different settings and participants, not a merged chronology or an identification of John's unnamed second disciple.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 4:18 As He walked beside the Sea of Galilee, He saw two brothers, Simon called Peter and Andrew his brother, casting a net into the sea, for they were fishermen.

    Matthew 4:19 He said to them, “Come after Me, and I will make you fishers of people.”

    Matthew 4:20 Immediately they left their nets and followed Him.

    Pinned target record

John 1:41

He first found his own brother Simon and said to him, “We have found the Messiah”—which, translated, is Christ.

  1. Matthew 16:16 narrative parallel

    Andrew announces to Simon that Jesus is the Messiah, explicitly translated Christ; Simon Peter later confesses Jesus as the Christ and Son of the living God. The matching Christ identification is compared across the brothers' distinct speeches, without adding Peter's fuller sonship language to Andrew's announcement.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 16:16 Simon Peter answered, “Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.”

  1. Matthew 16:17–16:18 narrative parallel

    Both scenes present Jesus addressing Simon in connection with the name Peter.     Jesus finds Philip and commands Follow Me; in Mark He addresses Levi at the tax station with the same command, and Levi follows. The concrete link is Jesus' individual summons to discipleship. Philip and Levi, their locations and the narrated responses are different and are not treated as one calling.

    Nicodemus responds to birth from above by asking about entering a mother's womb again; the woman at the well initially asks about drawing living water and avoiding physical thirst. Jesus answered, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from water and Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God.

  1. Ezekiel 36:25–36:27 allusion

    Jesus joins water and Spirit in the birth required for entrance into God's kingdom; Ezekiel promises cleansing water, a new heart and spirit, and God's Spirit within His people. These provide concrete scriptural renewal motifs as possible background. John does not explicitly quote Ezekiel here, and the comparison alone does not settle every interpretation of water or its ritual application.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 36:26 And I will give you a new heart, and I will give a new spirit within you; and I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh, and give you a heart of flesh.

John 3:6

What is born from the flesh is flesh, and what is born from the Spirit is spirit.

  1. Romans 8:5–8:9 teaching parallel

    Jesus distinguishes what originates from flesh and from Spirit; Romans contrasts flesh-directed and Spirit-directed life, with God's Spirit dwelling in believers. Both passages distinguish flesh and Spirit, but birth/origin and moral orientation are not identical explanations. John's statement is not expanded into a claim that physical embodiment itself is inherently evil.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 8:5 For those who are according to flesh set their minds on the things of the flesh, but those according to Spirit, on the things of the Spirit.

    Romans 8:7 because the mind of the flesh is hostility toward God; for it does not submit to God’s law, nor indeed can it.

    Romans 8:8 And those who are in flesh cannot please God.

    Romans 8:9 But you are not in flesh, but in Spirit, if indeed God’s Spirit dwells in you. But if anyone does not have Christ’s Spirit, that person does not belong to Him.

John 3:7

  1. John 3:3–3:5 teaching parallel

    Jesus explicitly recalls His saying about being born from above after the earlier requirement, Nicodemus's objection and the water-and-Spirit explanation. This is a deliberate return to the same birth teaching. The source's you all is preserved rather than restricting the requirement to Nicodemus alone or treating it as a separately reported conversation.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:3 Jesus answered and said to him, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from above, he cannot see the kingdom of God.”

    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”

    John 3:5 Jesus answered, “Amen, amen, I tell you: unless someone is born from water and Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God.

    Pinned target record

John 3:8

The wind blows where it wills, and you hear its sound, but you do not know where it comes from or where it goes; so it is with everyone born of the Spirit.”

  1. Ecclesiastes 11:5 thematic parallel

    Jesus compares Spirit-born people with the wind's audible but untraced movement; Ecclesiastes joins the unknown way of the spirit and bones in a pregnant womb with human inability to know God's works. Both connect untraceable divine activity with birth imagery. Ecclesiastes' supplied spirit wording is not silently changed to wind, nor are the two illustrations made one physical explanation.

    Read matching passage

    Ecclesiastes 11:5 among which no one knows the way of the spirit. As with bones in the womb of the pregnant woman, so you will not know God’s works, all that He will make.

    Pinned target record

John 3:9

Nicodemus answered Him, “How can these things happen?”

  1. Luke 1:34–1:35 thematic parallel

    Both how questions occur in birth conversations speaking of the Spirit, but Mary asks about conceiving the Son before the angel's explanation, whereas Nicodemus asks again after Jesus has taught birth from water and Spirit. This compares human questions amid divine birth agency while keeping virginal conception and the hearers' new birth distinct, along with speakers, order and responses.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 1:34 Mary said to the angel, “How will this be, since I do not know a man?”

    Luke 1:35 The angel answered her, “The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you; and so the holy One to be born will be called Son of God.

    Pinned target record

John 3:10

Jesus answered him, “Are you the teacher of Israel, and do you not understand these things?

  1. Ezekiel 36:25–36:27 thematic parallel

    Jesus challenges Israel's teacher about not understanding the preceding water-and-Spirit teaching; Ezekiel addresses Israel with promised cleansing water, a new heart and God's Spirit. The passage provides actual prophetic background relevant to that expectation of understanding. John does not name Ezekiel, so this is not a claim about Nicodemus's private syllabus or a proved direct quotation.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 36:25 I will sprinkle clean water upon you, and you will be cleansed from all your uncleannesses and all your idols. I will cleanse you.

    Ezekiel 36:26 And I will give you a new heart, and I will give a new spirit within you; and I will take the heart of stone out of your flesh, and give you a heart of flesh.

    Ezekiel 36:27 I will put My Spirit within you and cause you to walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.

    Pinned target record

John 3:11

Amen, amen, I tell you: we speak what we know and bear witness to what we have seen, and you all do not receive our testimony.

  1. John 3:32 teaching parallel

    Both sayings describe testimony grounded in what is known, seen or heard and resistance to receiving it. The earlier uses we and you all, while the later speaks of the heavenly One and no one receiving His testimony. These differing pronouns and emphases remain, with 3:33 immediately acknowledging an actual receiver rather than making rejection an exceptionless count.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:32 What He has seen and heard, this He testifies, and no one receives His testimony.

    Pinned target record

John 3:12

If I told you earthly things and you do not believe, how will you believe if I tell you heavenly things?

  1. Wisdom of Solomon 9:16–9:17 thematic parallel

    Jesus contrasts difficulty believing earthly teaching with the prospect of heavenly teaching; Wisdom says earthly matters are inferred only with difficulty and asks who traced heavenly things without God giving wisdom and sending His Holy Spirit. Both make the earthly/heavenly contrast about human reception of divine knowledge, with belief and knowing as distinct expressions rather than a claim of verbal quotation.

    Read matching passage

    Wisdom of Solomon 9:16 With difficulty we infer the things on earth, and find what lies at hand only with labor; but who has traced out the things in heaven?

    Wisdom of Solomon 9:17 And who knew Thy purpose, unless Thou didst give wisdom and send Thy Holy Spirit from on high?

    Pinned target record

John 3:13

And no one has ascended into heaven except the One who descended from heaven, the Son of Man.

  1. Ephesians 4:9–4:10 teaching parallel

    John joins the Son of Man's heavenly descent with His unique ascent; Ephesians identifies the descending One as the same One who ascended above all heavens. The concrete connection is Christ's descent-and-ascent pattern. Ephesians' lower-parts-of-the-earth phrase and filling-all-things purpose are additional and are not assigned a new geographic interpretation by this comparison.

    Read matching passage

    Ephesians 4:9 Now what does “He ascended” mean, except that He also descended into the lower parts of the earth?

    Ephesians 4:10 The One who descended is also the One who ascended far above all the heavens, so that He might fill all things.

    Pinned target record

John 3:14

And just as Moses lifted up the snake in the wilderness, so the Son of Man must be lifted up,

  1. Numbers 21:8–21:9 allusion

    Jesus expressly compares the Son of Man's being lifted up with Moses' lifted snake. Numbers records the bronze serpent set on a standard and bitten people looking at it and living. This supplies the specific wilderness episode, while John's ensuing belief-and-eternal-life application remains distinct from the narrated physical healing through looking.

    Read matching passage

    Numbers 21:8 And the Lord said unto Moses: Make a serpent for yourself, and set it upon a standard; and it shall be, if a serpent bites a man, that everyone bitten, when he looks upon it, shall live.

    Numbers 21:9 And Moses made a bronze serpent and set it on a standard. Whenever a serpent bit a person, that person looked at the bronze serpent and lived.

    Pinned target record

John 3:15

so that everyone who believes may have eternal life in Him.

  1. John 6:40 teaching parallel

    Both passages connect belief with eternal life in relation to the Son: John 3 speaks of life in Him, and John 6 of beholding the Son and believing in Him as the Father's will. The later promise to raise the believer on the last day is additional and is not inserted into the shorter earlier statement.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

    Pinned target record

John 3:16

For God loved the world in this way: He gave the only-begotten Son, so that everyone who believes in Him might not perish but have eternal life.

  1. 1 John 4:9–4:10 teaching parallel

    Both passages describe God's love expressed in giving or sending His Son into the world for people's life. The Gospel calls Him the only-begotten Son and describes belief versus perishing; the epistle calls Him His only Son and adds propitiation for sins. The matching divine-love-and-Son-gift pattern preserves those wording and explanatory differences.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 4:9 In this the love of God was manifested among us: God has sent His only Son into the world so that we might live through Him.

    1 John 4:10 In this is love: not that we have loved God, but that He loved us and sent His Son as propitiation for our sins.

    Pinned target record

John 3:17

For God did not send the Son into the world to judge the world, but so that the world might be saved through Him.

  1. John 12:47–12:48 teaching parallel

    Jesus' stated mission to save rather than judge the world recurs in the later discourse. John 12 also says His rejected word judges on the last day. The repeated saving-purpose statement is compared with its judgment context, without changing the earlier not-to-judge mission language into a claim that the Gospel contains no judgment.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:47 And if anyone hears My words and does not keep them, I do not judge him; for I did not come to judge the world, but to save the world.

    John 12:48 The one who rejects Me and does not receive My words has one who judges him: the word that I spoke will judge him on the last day.

John 3:18

The one who believes in Him is not judged; but the one who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God.

  1. John 5:24 teaching parallel

    John 3 contrasts belief in the Son without judgment with unbelief already judged; John 5 promises the hearer who believes the Sender eternal life and passage from death without coming into judgment. The shared faith-life-nonjudgment connection preserves belief in the Son versus in His Sender and present condemnation versus the later promise's formulation.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:24 Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who hears My word and believes the One who sent Me has eternal life, and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life.

    Pinned target record

John 3:19

And this is the judgment: the Light has come into the world, and people loved the darkness more than the Light, for their deeds were evil.

  1. John 1:9–1:11 teaching parallel

    The discourse explains judgment by the Light's coming into the world and people's preference for darkness; the prologue announces the Light coming and describes the world's nonrecognition and failure of reception. This is a recurring world-Light-response pattern. The discourse adds the evil-deeds explanation rather than making every clause a quotation of the prologue.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:9 The true Light, which gives light to every person, was coming into the world.

    John 1:10 He was in the world, and the world came into being through Him, and the world did not know Him.

    John 1:11 He came to what was His own, and His own did not receive Him.

    Pinned target record

John 3:20

For everyone who practices base things hates the light and does not come to the light, so that his deeds may not be exposed.

  1. Ephesians 5:11–5:13 teaching parallel

    Those doing base things avoid the light so their deeds are not exposed; Ephesians describes secret works of darkness and the exposing, making-visible action of light. Both use light as moral disclosure, while the exhortation to expose works in Ephesians and the explanation of avoidance in John have different roles.

    Read matching passage

    Ephesians 5:11 And do not share in the unfruitful works of darkness, but rather expose them,

    Ephesians 5:12 for it is shameful even to speak of the things done by them in secret.

    Ephesians 5:13 But all things, when exposed by the light, become visible,

    Pinned target record

John 3:21

But the one who does the truth comes to the light, so that his deeds may be manifested, because they have been done in God.”

  1. 1 John 1:6–1:7 teaching parallel

    John joins doing truth with coming to the light and God-wrought deeds; the epistle contrasts walking in darkness without practicing truth with walking in the light and fellowship. The recurring truth-and-light ethical pattern is concrete, while the epistle's fellowship and cleansing-by-Jesus'-blood statements remain additional.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 1:6 If we say that we have fellowship with Him while walking in darkness, we lie and do not practice the truth.

    1 John 1:7 But if we walk in the light, as He is in the light, we have fellowship with one another, and the blood of Jesus His Son cleanses us from every sin.

    Pinned target record

John 3:22

After these things Jesus and His disciples came into the Judean countryside, and there He stayed with them and was baptizing.

  1. John 4:1–4:2 narrative parallel

    John describes Jesus staying with disciples and baptizing; the later narrator reports His growing baptismal ministry and expressly clarifies that His disciples performed the baptisms rather than Jesus Himself. This is an internal clarification of agency within the same ministry, preserving the earlier attribution rather than editing its wording.

    Read matching passage

    John 4:1 So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples than John—

    John 4:2 although Jesus Himself was not baptizing, but His disciples—

John 3:23

John was also baptizing in Aenon near Salim, because there was much water there, and people came and were baptized.

  1. John 1:28 narrative parallel

    Both passages give a named setting for the same Baptist's activity: Aenon near Salim here and Bethany beyond the Jordan earlier. The comparison tracks the Gospel's baptismal-ministry locations, not their equivalence or modern coordinates. John 1 does not confirm Aenon's identity, its water supply or an unstated baptismal technique.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:28 These things happened in Bethany beyond the Jordan, where John was baptizing.

    Pinned target record

John 3:24

For John had not yet been thrown into prison.

  1. Mark 6:17 narrative parallel

    John places this baptismal scene before the Baptist's imprisonment; Mark explicitly describes Herod's seizing and binding John in prison. The later-life event provides the concrete temporal boundary John names. Mark's retrospective telling is not made the date of the arrest or used to move John's scene into another Gospel's sequence.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 6:17 For Herod himself had sent men to seize John and bind him in prison because of Herodias, the wife of his brother Philip, because he had married her.

John 3:25

Then a discussion arose between John’s disciples and a Jew concerning purification.

  1. John 2:6 thematic parallel

    John's disciples and a Jew dispute about purification amid the baptismal narrative; the earlier Cana description explicitly identifies water jars for Jewish purification. The shared purification subject gives a local contextual comparison, not a claim that the dispute concerned those jars or that its unstated arguments and particular rites can be reconstructed.

    Read matching passage

    John 2:6 Now six stone water jars were set there for the purification of the Jews, each holding two or three metretes.

John 3:26

And they came to John and said to him, “Rabbi, He who was with you beyond the Jordan, to whom you testified—behold, He is baptizing, and everyone is coming to Him.”

  1. John 4:1–4:2 narrative parallel

    John's disciples report that Jesus is baptizing and everyone is coming to Him; the later report heard by Pharisees says Jesus makes and baptizes more disciples than John and clarifies the disciples' agency. Both concern the comparative growth of the two ministries. Everyone in the earlier complaint is not converted into a literal count of all people everywhere.

    Read matching passage

    John 4:1 So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples than John—

    John 4:2 although Jesus Himself was not baptizing, but His disciples—

John 3:27

John answered, “A person can receive not even one thing unless it has been given to him from heaven.

  1. 1 Corinthians 4:7 teaching parallel

    John says a person can receive nothing unless it is given from heaven; Paul asks what anyone has that was not received and challenges boasting as if it were not a gift. Both oppose a self-originating claim to what is received, with John's ministry-response setting and Paul's factional boasting context kept distinct.

    Read matching passage

    1 Corinthians 4:7 For who makes you different? And what do you have that you did not receive? But if you did receive it, why do you boast as though you had not received it?

    Pinned target record

John 3:28

You yourselves bear witness for me that I said, ‘I am not the Christ, but I have been sent before Him.’

  1. John 1:20–1:23 narrative parallel

    John asks his hearers to witness his earlier denial of being the Christ and his preceding role; the earlier testimony explicitly denies the Christ title and identifies him as Isaiah's preparatory voice. This is a retrospective self-description, not a second independent commission or proof that every word of the later formulation appeared in the earlier quotation.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:20 And he confessed and did not deny, and confessed, “I am not the Christ.”

    John 1:21 They asked him, “What, then? Are you Elijah?” And he said, “I am not.” “Are you the Prophet?” And he answered, “No.”

    John 1:22 So they said to him, “Who are you, so that we may give an answer to those who sent us? What do you say about yourself?”

    John 1:23 He said, “I am a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Make straight the way of the Lord,’ just as Isaiah the prophet said.”

John 3:29

The one who has the bride is the bridegroom. But the bridegroom’s friend, who stands and hears him, rejoices greatly because of the bridegroom’s voice. So this joy of mine has been fulfilled.

  1. Mark 2:19–2:20 teaching parallel

    John compares Jesus' role with a bridegroom and his own joy with that of the bridegroom's friend; Jesus' fasting answer uses the bridegroom's presence with his companions and later removal. Both use wedding fellowship to explain response to Jesus. The Baptist's fulfilled joy and the disciples' fasting circumstances differ, without assigning every wedding figure an unstated identity.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 2:19 Jesus said to them, “Can the bridegroom’s companions fast while the bridegroom is with them? As long as they have the bridegroom with them, they cannot fast.

    Mark 2:20 But days will come when the bridegroom is taken away from them, and then they will fast on that day.

John 3:30

He must increase, but I must decrease.”

  1. Mark 1:7 teaching parallel

    John's increase/decrease saying places Jesus' prominence above his own; Mark's Baptist announces the stronger One after him and his unworthiness to untie His sandal. Both express the Baptist's subordinate role in relation to Jesus, without reducing the must to a mere attendance statistic or equating different sayings word for word.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:7 He proclaimed, saying, “The One stronger than I is coming after me. I am not worthy to stoop down and untie the strap of His sandals.

John 3:31

The One coming from above is above all. The one who is from the earth is from the earth and speaks from the earth. The One coming from heaven is above all.

  1. John 8:23 teaching parallel

    The passage contrasts the One coming from heaven with the one from earth; Jesus later contrasts His own being from above with His hearers' being from below and from this world. The origin-and-preeminence theme is shared, while the later direct address is not assigned to an unstated speaker in John 3's unquoted reflection.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:23 And He said to them, “You are from below; I am from above. You are from this world; I am not from this world.

    Pinned target record

John 3:32

What He has seen and heard, this He testifies, and no one receives His testimony.

  1. John 3:11 teaching parallel

    The testimony based on what the heavenly One has seen and heard repeats the earlier knowing/seeing/witnessing and nonreception pattern. The source's singular subject and no one wording differ from the earlier we and you all. The following 3:33 speaks of a receiver, so the rejection formula is not expanded into a denial of any believers.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:11 Amen, amen, I tell you: we speak what we know and bear witness to what we have seen, and you all do not receive our testimony.

    Pinned target record

John 3:33

The one who has received His testimony has set his seal that God is true.

  1. 1 John 5:9–5:10 teaching parallel

    Receiving the testimony affirms that God is true; the epistle describes God's testimony concerning His Son and says refusing to believe it makes God a liar. These are corresponding positive and negative responses to divine testimony. The seal image in John is not treated as an added physical ritual or a different person's identification.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 5:9 If we receive human testimony, the testimony of God is greater, because this is God’s testimony: He has borne witness concerning His Son.

    1 John 5:10 The one who believes in the Son of God has the testimony within himself. The one who does not believe God has made Him a liar, because he has not believed in the testimony God has borne concerning His Son.

    Pinned target record

John 3:34

For the One whom God sent speaks the words of God, for He gives the Spirit without measure.

  1. John 12:49–12:50 teaching parallel

    The One sent by God speaks God's words; Jesus later explicitly says the Father who sent Him commanded His speech and that He speaks as the Father told Him. This is a close sending-and-speaking connection. It supports that clause without assigning an unstated recipient or a new quantitative meaning to the additional Spirit-without-measure statement.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.

    John 12:50 And I know that His command is eternal life. So the things I speak, just as the Father has told Me, thus I speak.”

    Pinned target record

John 3:35

The Father loves the Son and has given all things into His hand.

  1. John 5:20 teaching parallel

    The Father loves the Son in both passages; John 5 explains that the Father shows Him all His own works and promises further works. This is an explicit recurring Father-Son love relation. The knowledge-and-works language there is not simply substituted for this unit's additional giving-all-things-into-His-hand clause.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:20 For the Father loves the Son and shows Him everything He Himself does, and He will show Him works greater than these, so that you may marvel.

    Pinned target record

  2. John 13:3 teaching parallel

    Both passages expressly state that the Father has given all things into Jesus' hand or hands. John 13 presents this as knowledge accompanying His later actions, while John 3 places it with the Father's love and the response to the Son. The matching grant language is preserved without claiming an identical discourse setting.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:3 knowing that the Father had given all things into His hands, and that He came out from God and was going to God,

John 3:36

The one who believes in the Son has eternal life; but the one who disobeys the Son will not see life, but the wrath of God remains upon him.

  1. 1 John 5:11–5:12 teaching parallel

    The Son is the decisive relation to having life in both passages: John 3 contrasts believing and disobeying the Son, while the epistle contrasts having and not having the Son. The shared life-in-the-Son teaching keeps those actual verbs and leaves John 3's remaining-wrath statement as its additional explicit warning.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 5:11 And this is the testimony: God gave us eternal life, and this life is in His Son.

    1 John 5:12 The one who has the Son has life; the one who does not have the Son of God does not have life.

John 4:1

So when Jesus learned that the Pharisees had heard that Jesus was making and baptizing more disciples than John—

    The earlier scene describes both baptizing ministries and John's followers reporting that everyone is coming to Jesus. John4:1 continues that growing-ministry setting with the Pharisees' hearing. The target does not itself narrate the Pharisees hearing or establish the numerical comparison as an independent census.

John 4:2

although Jesus Himself was not baptizing, but His disciples—

  1. John 3:22 internal narrative reference

    John3:22 attributes baptizing to Jesus while mentioning His disciples;4:2 expressly clarifies that the disciples, rather than Jesus personally, performed it. This is an internal agency clarification of the earlier narrative, not a second independent witness.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:22 After these things Jesus and His disciples came into the Judean countryside, and there He stayed with them and was baptizing.

John 4:3

He left Judea and went away again into Galilee.

  1. Matthew 4:12 bounded narrative comparison

    Both narrate Jesus withdrawing to Galilee. Matthew gives hearing of John's handover as the occasion; John places the departure after news of Jesus' baptizing ministry reaching the Pharisees. The different triggers and chronology are not merged into one asserted journey.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 4:12 When He heard that John had been handed over, He withdrew to Galilee.

John 4:4

And He had to pass through Samaria.

  1. Luke 9:51–9:53 narrative parallel

    Both journeys bring Jesus into a Samaritan setting, but John moves from Judea toward Galilee and its wider chapter gives a welcoming city, whereas Luke moves toward Jerusalem and gives a village's refusal. The specific regional-and-reception contrast does not identify the communities or explain the force of John's had to as a proved geographic or divine necessity.

    Luke 9:53 But they did not receive Him, because His face was set toward Jerusalem.

John 4:5

So He came to a city of Samaria called Sychar, near the plot of land Jacob gave to his son Joseph.

  1. Joshua 24:32 scriptural background comparison

    Both connect a field near Shechem/Sychar with Jacob's gift to Joseph. The actual Joshua unit says Jacob acquired the Shechem field and gave it to Joseph as a portion. John locates Sychar near the plot; the link does not equate the two city names or add identification of the well itself.

    Read matching passage

    Joshua 24:32 The sons of Israel brought Joseph’s bones up from Egypt and buried them at Shechem in the portion of the field that Jacob acquired from the Amorites living at Shechem for one hundred ewe lambs; he gave it to Joseph as a portion.

John 4:6

Jacob’s spring was there. Jesus, therefore, wearied from the journey, sat down as He was by the spring. It was about the sixth hour.

  1. John 19:28–19:30 thematic parallel

    John's well scene gives journey weariness followed by Jesus' request for a drink in 4:7; the passion scene gives His thirst and received sour wine. This is a concrete recurring bodily-need-and-drink motif. It does not establish Jacob's spring, the sixth-hour seating, an identical occasion or an unreported drink actually received at the well.

    Read matching passage

    John 19:28 After this, Jesus, knowing that all things had already been completed, says, so that the Scripture might be brought to completion, “I thirst.”

    John 19:30 When therefore Jesus had received the sour wine, He said, “It is finished.” And bowing His head, He gave up His spirit.

John 4:7

A woman from Samaria came to draw water. Jesus said to her, “Give Me a drink.”

  1. Genesis 24:17 bounded narrative comparison

    Both scenes have a request for drinking water addressed to a woman at a spring. Genesis' servant asks from Rebekah's jar; John has Jesus address the Samaritan woman. The concrete encounter pattern is compared without identifying the people or making the Samaritan encounter a marriage arrangement.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 24:17 And the servant ran to meet her, and said, “Give me a little water to drink from your water jar.”

John 4:8

For His disciples had gone away into the city to buy food.

  1. John 4:27–4:31 narrative parallel

    The disciples' earlier departure to buy food frames their later return and subsequent Rabbi, eat appeal around Jesus' conversation with the woman. The same group and food concern give a delayed internal narrative resumption, rather than a generic buying comparison. The later scene is not made an explicit receipt or account of what they successfully purchased.

John 4:9

So the Samaritan woman said to Him, “How dost Thou, being a Jew, ask a drink from me, a Samaritan woman?” For Jews do not associate with Samaritans.

  1. Luke 9:52–9:53 bounded narrative comparison

    Both depict a barrier between Jesus' Jewish/Jerusalem association and Samaritans. John records the woman's surprise at a Jew requesting her drink; Luke narrates another village refusing reception because of His Jerusalem destination. These are different communities and journeys; Luke does not prove a universal response by every Samaritan.

John 4:10

Jesus answered her, “If you knew the gift of God and who it is who says to you, ‘Give Me a drink,’ you would have asked Him, and He would have given you living water.”

  1. John 7:37–7:39 teaching or imagery comparison

    Both place the offer of living water in Jesus' speech. John7 additionally connects its water saying with the Spirit and Jesus' glorification;4:10 speaks of God's gift and knowing the speaker. The later explanation is a related Gospel passage, without inserting its whole formulation into this unit.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:37 On the last day, the great day of the feast, Jesus stood and cried out, saying, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to Me and drink.

    John 7:39 He said this concerning the Spirit, whom those who believed in Him were about to receive; for the Spirit was not yet, because Jesus had not yet been glorified.

John 4:11

The woman said to Him, “Sir, Thou hast no vessel for drawing water, and the well is deep. Where, then, dost Thou get this living water?

  1. John 3:4 teaching or imagery comparison

    The woman asks how Jesus can provide the promised water without a drawing vessel; Nicodemus asks how an old person can enter a womb and be born. Both dialogues place a tangible-mechanism question beside Jesus' life-giving imagery. The water and birth images and their questioners remain different.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”

    Pinned target record

John 4:12

Art Thou greater than our father Jacob, who gave us the well and drank from it himself, and his sons and his livestock?”

  1. John 8:53 teaching or imagery comparison

    Both interlocutors ask whether Jesus is greater than a named father: Jacob here and Abraham in8:53. John4 invokes the ancestral well, whereas John8 invokes death and Jesus' word. This is a repeated rhetorical challenge to Jesus' significance, not an identification of the patriarchs or settings.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:53 Art Thou greater than our father Abraham, who died? The prophets also died. Who dost Thou make Thyself out to be?”

    Pinned target record

John 4:13

Jesus answered her, “Everyone who drinks from this water will thirst again.

  1. John 6:49–6:50 teaching or imagery comparison

    John4 contrasts repeatedly needed well water with Jesus' enduring gift in the following verse. John6:49–50 contrasts manna eaten by ancestors who died with heaven's bread and life. The shared temporary/enduring provision contrast is retained; food, death, water and renewed thirst are not treated as identical assertions.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:49 Your fathers ate the manna in the wilderness and died.

    John 6:50 This is the bread that comes down from heaven, so that someone may eat from it and not die.

But whoever drinks from the water that I will give him will certainly never thirst; rather, the water I will give him will become in him a spring of water leaping up into eternal life.”

  1. John 6:35 teaching or imagery comparison

    Both promises explicitly say that the one receiving from Jesus will not thirst. John4 describes His water becoming a spring unto eternal life; John6 calls Him the bread of life and joins coming/believing with hunger/thirst. The different images remain visible.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:35 Jesus said to them, “I am the bread of life; the one who comes to Me will certainly never hunger, and the one who believes in Me will certainly never thirst.

    Pinned target record

John 4:15

The woman said to Him, “Sir, give me this water, so that I may not thirst or come here to draw.”

  1. John 6:34 teaching or imagery comparison

    The woman asks for the water Jesus has offered, just as John6's hearers ask Him always to give the offered bread. The water request adds avoiding thirst and the drawing trip; the bread request does not. This compares the dialogue responses, not identical physical provisions.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:34 So they said to Him, “Lord, always give us this bread.”

  1. John 4:17–4:18 internal command and marital disclosure

    The command to call a husband leads to the woman's no-husband reply and Jesus' distinction between five former husbands and her present relationship. This supplies the exchange's actual marital referent; it does not narrate a husband being fetched or claim an unstated complete motive for Jesus' request.

  1. John 4:18 narrative parallel

    Jesus' following for explanation explicitly supports His approval of the woman's no-husband statement: five earlier husbands are distinguished from the person she now has who is not her husband, ending again with speaking truly. This is a direct internal statement-and-explanation link, not identification of the men or approval of every feature of her unstated biography.

    Both conversations disclose Jesus' prior personal knowledge and lead into a response about His identity: Nathanael asks how He knows him and hears about the fig tree; the woman hears her marital history and next calls Him a prophet.     Both recipients of Jesus' disclosure or healing call Him a prophet. The Samaritan woman responds after His knowledge of her marital history; John9's healed man responds about the opening of his eyes. The two recognitions have different persons and evidential settings.

    Both narrate Jesus going up to Jerusalem in connection with a Jewish feast. John2 explicitly names Passover;5:1 does not name this feast. The recurring itinerary is compared without supplying a feast identity or treating the two visits as one.

  1. Nehemiah 3:1 scriptural background comparison

    Both name a Sheep Gate in the Jerusalem setting. Nehemiah records priests building and consecrating it; John locates the Bethzatha pool near it. The shared toponym does not prove unchanged masonry or supply John's pool name, five porticoes or precise archaeological identification.

    Both describe groups including blind and lame people in a healing context. Matthew's crowd brings sufferers to Jesus on the Galilean mountain; John's sufferers lie at a Jerusalem pool and the ensuing account concerns one man. Other conditions and the scope of healings are not made identical.

    Both stories specify a multi-year condition before Jesus' restoration in a Sabbath setting. The persons, durations, descriptions and causes remain different; no diagnosis is added to John's unit.

  1. Matthew 20:32 bounded narrative comparison

    Both have Jesus address prospective recipients with a question about the help desired. John asks one long-ill man whether he wants to become well; Matthew asks two blind men what they wish Him to do. The wording, number and initiative differ without identifying the encounters.

  1. Mark 2:3–2:4 bounded narrative comparison

    John's man lacks a helper to reach the pool; Mark's paralyzed man has four bearers who overcome a crowd and roof barrier. The concrete contrast concerns human assistance in access to healing. Mark supplies no pool mechanism, and the absent John5:4 angel account is not restored to explain the stirred water.

    Both commands tell a man to rise and take his mat. John adds walking; Mark tells the paralyzed man to go home. The Jerusalem-pool and Capernaum-house settings and patients remain different.

    The healed man reports the take-up-and-walk command actually spoken in5:8. This is an explicit internal quotation used in his response to the objection. It does not create a second independent witness or add a moral motive for his reply.

    Both healed men cannot provide requested information about an absent healer. John5's man does not know who He is; John9's man, who has already named Jesus, does not know where He is. The contrast preserves the different kinds of ignorance and does not claim the men had the same knowledge.

    Both healed men identify Jesus as responsible for their restoration when reporting to other people. John5 follows Jesus finding the man again; John9 recounts mud, washing and restored sight. The methods and patients differ, and no betrayal motive is assigned to John5's report.

    Both accounts narrate opposition to Jesus because He healed on Sabbath. John records persecution by the Jews in this scene; Luke records the synagogue ruler's indignation and crowd instruction.     Both relate Jesus' work to the divine Sender. John5 speaks of the Father working even now and Jesus working; John9 says we must work the Sender's works while it is day. The plural workers and day/night image in John9 are not made identical to John5's Sabbath defense.

John 5:18

  1. John 10:31–10:33 comparison of controversy narratives

    Both connect lethal hostility with Jesus' relation to God. John5:18 gives the narrator's explanation involving Sabbath breaking and calling God His own Father, making Himself equal to God; John10:33 quotes opponents accusing Him of blasphemy and making Himself God. The different formulations and narrative attribution remain distinct, without adding a separate doctrinal ruling.

    Both sayings deny autonomous action or speech from Jesus Himself and refer it to the Father. John5 concerns the Son doing what He sees the Father do; John12 concerns speaking what the sending Father commanded. Doing and speaking are related but not interchangeable formulations.

    Both attribute life-giving power to Jesus/the Son. John5 compares the Son's giving life with the Father raising the dead; John11 names Jesus resurrection and life and promises life to the believer despite death. Their qualifications are retained rather than replaced by a universal timetable.

    John11 narrates Jesus' loud command followed by the dead Lazarus coming out, a concrete voice-and-life counterpart. John5 speaks more broadly of the dead hearing the Son and living. One bodily restoration does not by itself settle the full meaning, scope or coming/now-is timing of that saying.

    Both passages distinguish outcomes among those arising from death.     Both deny pursuing Jesus' own will in favor of the will of the One who sent Him. John5 applies this to hearing and righteous judgment; John6 frames His heavenly descent and mission. The shared will statement does not make their full subjects identical.

John 5:31

If I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is not true.

  1. John 8:13–8:18 teaching or imagery comparison

    Both passages explicitly discuss Jesus bearing witness concerning Himself. John5's conditional says His testimony is not true; John8 affirms its truth and adds the Father and the two-person testimony rule. The differing formulations are left visible rather than silently changing true into another term or forcing a complete harmonization.

    Both introduce witness beyond Jesus' own testimony. John8 explicitly names the sending Father; the present John5 unit says Another without naming Him.     Both portray Jesus as knowing people's inner state. John5 specifies their lack of God's love; John2 says He knew what was within a person and did not entrust Himself to the sign-believing group. The target does not independently name or diagnose every John5 listener.

    Both describe Jesus' coming and failure to receive Him.     Acts explicitly places Jesus the appointed Christ beside Moses' promised prophet and the command to hear him. It provides a specific Mosaic-prophecy comparison for Jesus' saying that Moses wrote concerning Him. John5 does not name this as the only Mosaic text intended.

John 5:47

  1. Luke 16:29–16:31 teaching or imagery comparison

    Both sayings connect refusal of Moses' witness with failure to receive a further disclosure. John5 contrasts Moses' writings and Jesus' words; Luke's Abraham contrasts hearing Moses/Prophets with persuasion by someone risen. The parable's speaker and resurrection scenario remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 16:29 But Abraham said, ‘They have Moses and the Prophets; let them hear them.’

I am the living bread that came down from heaven. If anyone eats from this bread, he will live forever. And the bread that I will give is My flesh, for the life of the world.”

  1. Luke 22:19 body-and-bread comparison

    Both connect given bread with Jesus' body or flesh for others. Luke gives the supper's body-given-for-you and remembrance words; John gives flesh for the world's life. Distinct scenes and terms do not settle every ritual interpretation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 22:19 And He took bread, and having given thanks, He broke it and gave it to them, saying, “This is My body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of Me.”

John 6:52

  1. John 3:4 same-book response comparison

    Both questioners ask how Jesus' life-giving teaching can be physically understood. Nicodemus asks about returning to a womb; this group asks about flesh to eat. Their images, identities and subsequent explanations remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:4 Nicodemus said to Him, “How can a person be born when he is old? Can he enter his mother’s womb a second time and be born?”

John 6:54

The one who eats My flesh and drinks My blood has eternal life, and I will raise him up on the last day.

  1. John 6:40 internal teaching parallel

    Both promise eternal life and raising on the last day. Here the terms are eating flesh and drinking blood; earlier they are beholding the Son and believing. The stated actions are preserved rather than automatically substituted.

John 6:55

  1. 1 Corinthians 10:16 body-and-blood comparison

    Both relate Christ's body or flesh and blood to shared food and drink. Paul names bread and the blessing cup as participation; John calls flesh true food and blood true drink. Neither account supplies all the other's expressions.

    Read matching passage

    1 Corinthians 10:16 The cup of blessing that we bless—is it not participation in the blood of Christ? The bread that we break—is it not participation in the body of Christ?
