  1. John 15:4–15:5 same-book teaching parallel

    Both describe mutual remaining in Jesus and His followers. John 6 connects it with flesh and blood; John 15 uses vine, branches and fruit. The images are not turned into identical physical descriptions.

    John 15:5 I am the vine; you are the branches. The one who remains in Me, and I in him, bears much fruit, because apart from Me you can do nothing.

John 6:57

This is the bread that came down from heaven, not as the fathers ate and died. The one who eats this bread will live forever.”

    This conclusion reprises the contrast between ancestors who ate and died and Jesus' heavenly bread giving enduring life. The earlier units contain that contrast and identify His flesh, without independently attesting another discourse.

    Peter's reply to the question about leaving names Jesus' words of eternal life and the group's confession; Jesus then qualifies the Twelve's position by speaking of one devil, identified as Judas. This explains the narrative response without asserting every individual had Peter's faith or predicting that no disciple would depart.

John 7:30

So they sought to seize Him, but no one laid a hand on Him, because His hour had not yet come.

  1. John 8:20 same-book timing recurrence

    Both explain failure to seize Jesus by His hour not yet coming. John 8 locates its saying at the treasury; John 7 follows temple origin-and-sending teaching. They are recurring notices, not one event.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:20 He spoke these words at the treasury as He taught in the temple, and no one seized Him, because His hour had not yet come.

    Pinned target record

John 7:31

But many from the crowd believed in Him and said, “When the Christ comes, will He do more signs than this man has done?”

  1. John 2:23 same-book sign-response comparison

    Both describe many believing in Jesus in relation to signs at a feast. John 7 includes the crowd's question about greater future signs; John 2 names belief in His name. Their groups and maturity are not presumed identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 2:23 While He was in Jerusalem at the Passover, during the feast, many believed in His name, seeing the signs He was doing.

    Pinned target record

John 7:32

The Pharisees heard the crowd grumbling these things about Him, and the chief priests and the Pharisees sent officers to seize Him.

  1. John 11:57 same-book arrest-plan comparison

    Both name chief priests and Pharisees arranging Jesus' seizure. John 7 sends officers; John 11 seeks information about His whereabouts. The methods and occasions differ.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:57 The chief priests and the Pharisees had given orders that if anyone knew where He was, he should disclose it, so that they might seize Him.

    Pinned target record

John 7:33

So Jesus said, “I am with you for a little time longer, and I go to the One who sent Me.

  1. John 16:5 same-book departure teaching

    Both speak of Jesus going to the One who sent Him. John 7 adds a little time longer; John 16 addresses disciples and their failure to ask His destination.

    Read matching passage

    John 16:5 But now I am going to the One who sent Me, and none of you asks Me, ‘Where art Thou going?’

    Pinned target record

John 7:34

You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

  1. John 13:33 internal teaching callback

    Jesus later explicitly recalls telling the Jews that where He goes they cannot come and applies the saying to disciples. The changing audience is preserved, without imposing identical or permanent exclusion on both groups.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:33 Little children, I am with you a little longer. You will seek Me, and just as I told the Jews, ‘Where I go, you cannot come,’ I now tell you also.

    Pinned target record

John 7:35

So the Jews said among themselves, “Where is this man about to go that we will not find Him? Is He about to go to the Dispersion among the Greeks and teach the Greeks?

  1. John 12:20–12:22 narrative parallel

    The hearers speculate that Jesus might go to the Greek Dispersion and teach Greeks; later Greek visitors approach through Philip and Andrew to seek Jesus. This contrasts imagined outward travel with visitors approaching Him. It does not narrate the speculated journey, identify the two groups or claim the later requested audience is explicitly reported as granted.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:20 Now there were some Greeks among those going up to worship at the feast.

    John 12:21 These approached Philip, who was from Bethsaida of Galilee, and asked him, saying, “Sir, we want to see Jesus.”

    John 12:22 Philip came and told Andrew; Andrew and Philip came and told Jesus.

    Pinned target record

John 7:36

What is this saying He spoke: ‘You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come’?”

  1. John 7:34 internal quotation reference

    The question repeats Jesus' immediately preceding seek/not-find and cannot-come saying. This identifies the quoted statement, not an independent verification of the hearers' proposed destination or future events.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:34 You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

    Pinned target record

John 7:37

On the last day, the great day of the feast, Jesus stood and cried out, saying, “If anyone thirsts, let him come to Me and drink.

  1. Isaiah 55:1 possible allusion

    Both invite thirsty people to receive provision. Isaiah summons them to water and freely obtained food and drink; Jesus invites coming to Him and drinking. John does not name Isaiah or repeat the full invitation.

    Read matching passage

    Isaiah 55:1 You who thirst, go to the water; and you who have no silver, go and buy wine and rich food, and drink without silver or price.

    Pinned target record

John 7:38

The one who believes in Me—as the Scripture said, rivers of living water will flow from his belly.”

  1. Zechariah 14:8 possible imagery background

    Both describe flowing living water. Zechariah locates it in Jerusalem; John relates rivers from his belly to believing in Jesus. The comparison does not identify the sources or prove this is John's sole scriptural referent.

    Read matching passage

    Zechariah 14:8 On that day living water will go out from Jerusalem, half into the first sea and half into the last sea; it will be so in summer and spring.

    Pinned target record

John 7:39

He said this concerning the Spirit, whom those who believed in Him were about to receive; for the Spirit was not yet, because Jesus had not yet been glorified.

  1. Acts 2:33 Spirit-reception comparison

    Both relate Spirit reception or outpouring to Jesus' glorification or exaltation. Acts describes the exalted Jesus receiving the Father's promise and pouring it out; John's Spirit-was-not-yet wording is retained rather than expanded.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 2:33 Therefore, exalted to God’s right hand and having received from the Father the promised Holy Spirit, He has poured out what you both see and hear.

    Pinned target record

Reading note on John 7:39 — editorial. “The Spirit was not yet” concerns the giving or reception of the Spirit in relation to Jesus’ glorification, as the surrounding verse explains; it is not a statement that the Holy Spirit did not exist. Some English translations supply “given.” The reading text preserves the selected Greek wording. See the linked comparison edition for its textual explanation. This note is separate from the verse.

John 7:40

So some from the crowd, hearing these words, said, “This is truly the Prophet.”

  1. John 6:14 same-book title recurrence

    Both crowds identify Jesus as truly the Prophet. John 6 follows the bread sign; John 7 follows the water discourse. The speakers and occasions are not assumed identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:14 When the people saw the sign He did, they said, “This is truly the Prophet who is coming into the world.”

    Pinned target record

John 7:41

Others said, “This is the Christ.” But some said, “Surely the Christ does not come from Galilee?

  1. John 1:45–1:46 same-book origin-objection comparison

    Both scenes question Jesus through perceived geographic origin. Nathanael questions Nazareth; the feast crowd questions Galilee and divides over Christ identification. The characters' premises are not endorsed as the narrator's geography.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”

    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

    Pinned target record

John 7:42

Has not the Scripture said that the Christ comes from the offspring of David and from Bethlehem, the village where David was?”

  1. Micah 5:1 scriptural expectation background

    The crowd invokes Bethlehem as the ruler's expected origin; Micah addresses Bethlehem and announces Israel's ruler from it. The target supplies background for the claim, not John's independent narration of Jesus' birthplace.

    Read matching passage

    Micah 5:1 And you, Bethlehem, house of Ephrathah, are small to be among Judah’s thousands; from you one will come out for Me to be a ruler in Israel, and his goings out are from the beginning, from days of old.

    Pinned target record

  2. 2 Samuel 7:12–7:13 scriptural expectation background

    The crowd's Davidic-seed expectation corresponds to God's promise of David's seed and an enduring throne. Samuel does not name Bethlehem in these units or state this crowd's knowledge of Jesus' genealogy.

    Read matching passage

    2 Samuel 7:12 When your days are fulfilled and you sleep with your fathers, I will raise up your seed after you, who will be from your own body, and prepare his kingdom.

    2 Samuel 7:13 He will build Me a house for My name, and I will set his throne upright for ever.

    Pinned target record

John 7:43

So a division arose in the crowd because of Him.

  1. John 9:16 same-book division recurrence

    Both explicitly report division over Jesus. John 7 concerns Messiah and origin claims; John 9 concerns Sabbath observance and healing signs. The divided groups are not automatically identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 9:16 Then some of the Pharisees said, “This man is not from God, because He does not keep the Sabbath.” But others said, “How can a sinful man do such signs?” And there was division among them.

    Pinned target record

John 7:44

Some of them wanted to seize Him, but no one laid hands on Him.

  1. John 10:39 same-book arrest comparison

    Both describe efforts to seize Jesus without completed custody. John 7 says no one laid hands on Him; John 10 says He went out from their hand. The exact circumstances are not made identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 10:39 So they sought again to seize Him, and He went out from their hand.

    Pinned target record

John 7:45

So the officers came to the chief priests and Pharisees, and these said to them, “Why did you not bring Him?”

  1. John 7:32 internal mission callback

    The officers' return and question about not bringing Jesus refer back to their earlier seizure commission from priests and Pharisees. The later notice reports failure to complete it, not another independently attested arrest mission.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:32 The Pharisees heard the crowd grumbling these things about Him, and the chief priests and the Pharisees sent officers to seize Him.

    Pinned target record

John 7:46

The officers answered, “Never has a person spoken in this way.”

  1. Mark 1:22 teaching-response comparison

    Both describe exceptional response to Jesus' speech. Officers say no person spoke this way; Mark's hearers marvel at authoritative teaching unlike scribes. The listeners and settings differ.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 1:22 They were astonished at His teaching, for He taught them as one having authority, and not as the scribes.

    Pinned target record

John 7:47

So the Pharisees answered them, “Have you also been led astray?

  1. John 7:12 internal accusation recurrence

    The Pharisees' led-astray question repeats the misleading accusation voiced in the crowd's earlier debate. Different speakers use the charge; neither occurrence makes it the narrator's judgment about Jesus.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:12 And there was much grumbling about Him among the crowds. Some said, “He is good,” but others said, “No, He leads the crowd astray.”

    Pinned target record

John 7:48

Has any of the rulers believed in Him, or any of the Pharisees?

  1. John 12:42 same-book response contrast

    The rhetorical question about rulers' belief is compared with the later report of many rulers believing but fearing public confession. Different times and public/private responses do not establish when each ruler first believed.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:42 Nevertheless, many even from the rulers believed in Him, but because of the Pharisees they did not confess Him, so that they might not be put out of the synagogue,

    Pinned target record

John 7:49

But this crowd that does not know the Law is accursed.”

  1. John 9:34 same-book hostile-response comparison

    Both record disparagement of people favorable to Jesus. Here the crowd is called accursed and law-ignorant; later a healed man is called sin-born and unfit to teach. The reported accusations are not endorsed.

    Read matching passage

    John 9:34 They answered him, “You were born entirely in sins, and do you teach us?” And they cast him out.

    Pinned target record

John 7:50

Nicodemus, who had come to Him earlier and was one of them, said to them,

  1. John 3:1–3:2 internal person reference

    The earlier Nicodemus visit identifies this returning participant as a Pharisee and ruler who came at night. This does not make his later legal question a record of every earlier belief.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:1 Now there was a man from the Pharisees named Nicodemus, a ruler of the Jews.

    John 3:2 He came to Him at night and said to Him, “Rabbi, we know that Thou hast come from God as a teacher, for no one can do these signs that Thou doest unless God is with him.”

    Pinned target record

John 7:51

“Does our Law judge a person unless it first hears from him and knows what he does?”

  1. Deuteronomy 1:16–1:17 legal background

    Moses directs judges to hear disputes and judge righteously without partiality. Nicodemus invokes prior hearing and knowledge before judgment. The rule supplies background, not a proved verbatim citation of a particular statute.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 1:16 And I commanded your judges at that time, saying: Hear the matters between your brethren, and judge justly between a man and his brother and the sojourner with him.

    Deuteronomy 1:17 You shall not show partiality in judgment; you shall judge the small and the great alike. You shall not shrink before the face of man, for judgment belongs unto God. And you shall bring unto me the matter which is too hard for you, and I will hear it.

    Pinned target record

John 7:52

They answered him, “Are you also from Galilee? Search and see that a prophet does not arise from Galilee.”

  1. John 1:45–1:46 thematic parallel

    The Galilee-prophet objection to Nicodemus is compared with Nathanael's earlier objection about good coming from Nazareth and Philip's invitation to come and see. Both challenge a claim through regional origin. The places and addressees are not conflated, and the comparison does not independently disprove the Galilee claim by assigning a prophet modern geographic coordinates.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:45 Philip found Nathanael and said to him, “We have found the One of whom Moses wrote in the Law, and the prophets also: Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth.”

    John 1:46 Nathanael said to him, “Can anything good come from Nazareth?” Philip said to him, “Come and see.”

    Pinned target record

John 8:1

But Jesus went to the Mount of Olives.

  1. Luke 21:37 narrative parallel

    The double-bracketed John sequence sends Jesus to the Mount of Olives. Luke describes His lodging there at night during a Temple-teaching period. The shared movement does not establish the same night or the same narrative setting.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 21:37 By day He was teaching in the temple, but at night He went out and lodged on the mount called Olivet.

    Pinned target record

John 8:2

At dawn He came again into the temple, and all the people were coming to Him; and He sat down and taught them.

  1. Luke 21:37–21:38 narrative parallel

    Both describe Jesus teaching in the Temple and people coming early to hear Him. John gives a particular dawn arrival and seated teaching within its double-bracketed sequence; Luke summarizes a recurring daily pattern.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 21:37 By day He was teaching in the temple, but at night He went out and lodged on the mount called Olivet.

    Luke 21:38 And all the people would come early in the morning to Him in the temple to hear Him.

    Pinned target record

John 8:3

And the scribes and the Pharisees brought a woman caught in adultery, and set her in the midst.

  1. Exodus 20:13 thematic parallel

    John identifies the woman's alleged offense as adultery; the saved Exodus command prohibits adultery. This supplies the law's moral background, not this woman's identity, the evidence against her, or the prescribed penalty.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 20:13 You shall not commit adultery.

    Pinned target record

John 8:4

They said to Him, “Teacher, this woman was caught in the very act of committing adultery.

  1. Deuteronomy 22:22 thematic parallel

    The reported caught-in-adultery charge is compared with the legal case of a man found lying with another man's wife. Deuteronomy explicitly concerns both parties and death; this unit does not specify stoning. The comparison supplies legal offense background without inventing the woman's marital details, the absent man's fate or an additional verdict on her case.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 22:22 And if a man is found lying with a woman married unto a husband, you shall put both of them to death, the man who lay with the woman, and the woman; and you shall remove the evil one from Israel.

    Pinned target record

John 8:5

Now in the Law Moses commanded us that such women be stoned. What, then, dost Thou say?”

  1. Deuteronomy 22:22–22:24 allusion

    The accusers appeal to Moses concerning stoning. Deuteronomy orders death for both adulterous parties and, in its betrothed-city case, stoning of both. John does not supply all those case conditions or authorize restricting the law to women alone.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 22:22 And if a man is found lying with a woman married unto a husband, you shall put both of them to death, the man who lay with the woman, and the woman; and you shall remove the evil one from Israel.

    Deuteronomy 22:23 And if a young woman, a virgin, is betrothed unto a man, and a man finds her in the city and lies with her,

    Deuteronomy 22:24 you shall bring both of them forth unto the gate of their city, and they shall be stoned with stones and shall die: the young woman, because she did not cry out in the city, and the man, because he violated his neighbour’s wife. And you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.

    Pinned target record

John 8:6

They said this to test Him, so that they might have something with which to accuse Him. But Jesus bent down and wrote with His finger on the ground.

  1. Matthew 22:15–22:18 thematic parallel

    Both narratives explicitly describe a question meant to trap or test Jesus. Matthew concerns tax and a verbal snare; John's question concerns adultery and accusation, with ground-writing absent from Matthew. These are different confrontations.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 22:15 Then the Pharisees went and took counsel how to trap Him in a statement.

    Matthew 22:16 They sent Him their disciples with the Herodians, saying, “Teacher, we know that Thou art truthful and dost teach God’s way in truth. Thou art not swayed by anyone, for Thou dost not look at people’s outward status.

    Matthew 22:17 Tell us, then, what Thou thinkest: is it lawful to give tribute to Caesar, or not?”

    Matthew 22:18 Knowing their evil intent, Jesus said, “Why do you test Me, hypocrites?

    Pinned target record

John 8:7

As they persisted in questioning Him, He straightened up and said to them, “Let the one among you who is without sin be first to cast a stone at her.”

  1. Deuteronomy 17:6–17:7 allusion

    Deuteronomy requires multiple witnesses and the witnesses' hands first in an execution. Jesus calls for the sinless person among these accusers to cast the first stone. The shared first-action imagery does not make Deuteronomy itself require sinlessness.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 17:6 Upon the testimony of two witnesses or three witnesses shall he who is condemned to death be put to death; he shall not die upon the testimony of one witness.

    Deuteronomy 17:7 And the hand of the witnesses shall be first upon him to put him to death, and afterward the hand of all the people; and you shall remove the evil one from among yourselves.

    Pinned target record

John 8:8

And again He bent down and wrote on the ground.

  1. Exodus 31:18 allusion

    John repeats Jesus' writing on the ground, previously described as with His finger. Exodus describes stone tablets written with God's finger. This is a possible writing-and-law imagery comparison; John never identifies what Jesus wrote or equates it with the tablets.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 31:18 When He finished speaking with Moses on Mount Sinai, He gave him the two tablets of the testimony, stone tablets written with the finger of God.

    Pinned target record

John 8:9

But when they heard, they went out one by one, beginning with the older ones. And Jesus was left alone, and the woman standing in the midst.

  1. John 8:7 narrative parallel

    The earlier preserved instruction tells the sinless person to throw first; the later narration marks the departure as occurring when they heard. This supplies the actual spoken antecedent without adding a conscience clause, stating each accuser's inner guilt or explaining why the older ones left first.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:7 As they persisted in questioning Him, He straightened up and said to them, “Let the one among you who is without sin be first to cast a stone at her.”

    Pinned target record

John 8:10

Jesus straightened up and said to her, “Woman, where are they? Has no one condemned you?”

  1. John 8:11 internal condemnation question and answer

    The woman's No one answers Jesus' condemnation question, followed by His own refusal to condemn and instruction to sin no more. This is the same double-bracketed discourse's answer, not another absolution event, an identification of the unnamed woman or a claim that the text reports every accuser's motive.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:11 She said, “No one, Lord.” Jesus said, “Neither do I condemn you. Go, and from now on sin no more.”⟧

    Pinned target record

John 8:11

She said, “No one, Lord.” Jesus said, “Neither do I condemn you. Go, and from now on sin no more.”⟧

  1. John 5:14 thematic parallel

    Jesus tells both addressed people to sin no more. John 8 follows the accusers' departure and His refusal to condemn the woman; John 5 follows healing and includes a warning of something worse. The people and forms of deliverance differ.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:14 After these things Jesus found him in the temple and said to him, “Behold, you have become well. Sin no more, so that something worse may not happen to you.”

    Pinned target record

John 8:12

Then Jesus spoke to them again, saying, “I am the Light of the world. The one who follows Me will certainly not walk in the darkness, but will have the light of life.”

  1. John 1:3–1:5 thematic parallel

    Both join life and light against darkness in the presentation of Jesus. The prologue speaks through creation and Word imagery; John 8 speaks of following Jesus as the world's Light. The prologue's continuation is retained rather than detached from its preceding clause.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:3 All things came into being through Him, and without Him not one thing came into being. What has come into being

    John 1:4 in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.

    John 1:5 And the light shines in the darkness, and the darkness did not overcome it.

    Pinned target record

John 8:13

So the Pharisees said to Him, “Thou bearest witness concerning Thyself; Thy testimony is not true.”

  1. John 5:31 thematic parallel

    The opponents' self-witness objection repeats the earlier self-witness/not-true formulation. In John 5 Jesus states a conditional before discussing other witnesses; in John 8 opponents make the charge and Jesus answers it. The speakers and argumentative settings differ.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:31 If I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is not true.

    Pinned target record

John 8:14

Jesus answered them, “Even if I bear witness concerning Myself, My testimony is true, because I know where I came from and where I am going. But you do not know where I come from or where I am going.

  1. John 13:3–13:4 thematic parallel

    Both describe Jesus knowing where He comes from and where He goes. John 8 makes that knowledge a ground for His testimony; John 13 places coming from God and returning to God before the foot-washing. These are different scenes.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:3 knowing that the Father had given all things into His hands, and that He came out from God and was going to God,

    John 13:4 He rose from supper and laid aside His garments, and took a towel and tied it around Himself.

    Pinned target record

John 8:15

You judge according to the flesh; I judge no one.

  1. John 12:47–12:48 thematic parallel

    Both contain Jesus' statement that He does not judge in the described situation. John 8 contrasts His hearers' judgment according to flesh; John 12 explains His saving mission and the word's last-day judgment. This does not erase the Gospel's other judgment sayings.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:47 And if anyone hears My words and does not keep them, I do not judge him; for I did not come to judge the world, but to save the world.

    John 12:48 The one who rejects Me and does not receive My words has one who judges him: the word that I spoke will judge him on the last day.

    Pinned target record

John 8:16

But even if I judge, My judgment is true, because I am not alone, but I and the Father who sent Me.

  1. John 5:30 thematic parallel

    Both ground Jesus' judgment in His relationship to the Father who sends Him. John 8 says He is not alone and His judgment is true; John 5 speaks of hearing, righteous judgment, and seeking the Sender's will. The explanations are related but differently worded.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:30 I can do nothing from Myself. As I hear, I judge, and My judgment is righteous, because I do not seek My own will, but the will of the One who sent Me.

    Pinned target record

John 8:17

And in your own Law it is written that the testimony of two people is true.

  1. Deuteronomy 19:15 quotation

    Jesus invokes the law's two-person testimony rule. Deuteronomy says one witness is insufficient and two or three establish a matter. John's formulation is not a claim that any two agreeing people automatically speak factual truth.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 19:15 One witness shall not suffice to testify against a man concerning any injustice, or any trespass, or any sin which he sins. At the mouth of two witnesses, and at the mouth of three witnesses, shall every matter be established.

    Pinned target record

John 8:18

I am the One who bears witness concerning Myself, and the Father who sent Me bears witness concerning Me.”

  1. John 5:37 thematic parallel

    Both identify the Father who sent Jesus as bearing witness about Him. John 8 also names Jesus' own testimony; John 5 adds that its hearers have not heard the Father's voice or seen His form. The witness lists are not identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:37 And the Father who sent Me has Himself testified concerning Me. You have never heard His voice or seen His form,

    Pinned target record

John 8:19

So they said to Him, “Where is Thy Father?” Jesus answered, “You know neither Me nor My Father. If you knew Me, you would also know My Father.”

  1. John 14:7–14:9 thematic parallel

    Both link knowing Jesus with knowing the Father. John 8 answers a question about where His Father is; John 14 answers Philip's request to be shown the Father and uses seeing language as well. Knowing and seeing are not silently made identical expressions.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:7 If you have known Me, you will also know My Father. From now on you know Him and have seen Him.”

    John 14:8 Philip says to Him, “Lord, show us the Father, and it is enough for us.”

    John 14:9 Jesus says to him, “Have I been with you for so long a time, and you have not known Me, Philip? The one who has seen Me has seen the Father. How can you say, ‘Show us the Father’?

    Pinned target record

John 8:20

He spoke these words at the treasury as He taught in the temple, and no one seized Him, because His hour had not yet come.

  1. John 7:30 thematic parallel

    Both explain a failed attempt to seize Jesus by saying His hour had not yet come. John 8 locates teaching at the treasury; John 7 belongs to an earlier confrontation. This is an internal repeated explanation, not independent attestation of one arrest attempt.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:30 So they sought to seize Him, but no one laid a hand on Him, because His hour had not yet come.

    Pinned target record

John 8:21

So He said to them again, “I am going away, and you will seek Me, and you will die in your sin. Where I go, you cannot come.”

  1. John 7:33–7:34 thematic parallel

    Jesus again speaks of going away, being sought, and hearers being unable to come where He goes. John 8 adds dying in their sin; John 7 speaks of not finding Him and going to the Sender. The warnings retain their different wording.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:33 So Jesus said, “I am with you for a little time longer, and I go to the One who sent Me.

    John 7:34 You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

    Pinned target record

John 8:22

So the Jews said, “Will He kill Himself, since He says, ‘Where I go, you cannot come’?”

  1. John 7:35–7:36 thematic parallel

    Both report hearers proposing an explanation for Jesus' inaccessible destination. John 7 asks about the Greek Dispersion; John 8 asks about suicide. These reported guesses are not endorsed as the narrator's account of Jesus' destination.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:35 So the Jews said among themselves, “Where is this man about to go that we will not find Him? Is He about to go to the Dispersion among the Greeks and teach the Greeks?

    John 7:36 What is this saying He spoke: ‘You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come’?”

    Pinned target record

John 8:23

And He said to them, “You are from below; I am from above. You are from this world; I am not from this world.

  1. John 3:31 thematic parallel

    Both contrast an origin from above or heaven with an earthly origin. John 8 applies above/below and world language directly to Jesus and His hearers; John 3 contrasts the heavenly One's testimony with speech from the earth.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:31 The One coming from above is above all. The one who is from the earth is from the earth and speaks from the earth. The One coming from heaven is above all.

    Pinned target record

John 8:24

Therefore I told you that you will die in your sins; for unless you believe that I am, you will die in your sins.”

  1. John 3:18 thematic parallel

    Both connect unbelief with an adverse outcome. John 8 speaks of dying in sins unless the hearers believe that Jesus is 'I am'; John 3 speaks of judgment and belief in the only-begotten Son's name. Their distinct identity formulas are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    John 3:18 The one who believes in Him is not judged; but the one who does not believe has already been judged, because he has not believed in the name of the only-begotten Son of God.

    Pinned target record

John 8:25

So they said to Him, “Who art Thou?” Jesus said to them, “Why do I even speak to you at all?

  1. John 10:24–10:25 narrative parallel

    Both identity demands receive a reply challenging the hearers' reception of Jesus' speech. John8 preserves Why do I even speak to you at all; John10 says He told them and they do not believe, adding the witness of works. The first reply is not replaced by a familiar from-the-beginning rendering.

    Read matching passage

    John 10:24 So the Jews surrounded Him and said to Him, “How long wilt Thou keep our soul in suspense? If Thou art the Christ, tell us openly.”

    Both connect God's truth and sending with the words Jesus speaks. John 3 describes accepting testimony and the sent One speaking God's words; John 8 describes speaking to the world what He heard from the true Sender. Their surrounding judgment and Spirit statements differ.

    Read matching passage

  1. John 10:6 thematic parallel

    Both narrator comments explicitly state that Jesus' hearers did not understand. John 8 concerns His reference to the Father; John 10 concerns a sheep-related figure of speech. This is an internal narrative pattern with different subjects and occasions.

    Read matching passage

  1. John 12:32–12:33 thematic parallel

    Both use Jesus' being lifted up in relation to His mission. John 12 explicitly explains the expression as indicating the manner of His death; John 8 links lifting up the Son of Man with recognition and His Father-taught speech. The explanations are not word-for-word duplicates.

    Both affirm that Jesus is not alone because the Father is with Him. John 8 adds that He always does what pleases the Father; John 16 contrasts the Father's presence with the disciples' coming abandonment. The human circumstances differ.

    Both link remaining in Jesus or His words with discipleship. John 8 says remaining in His word marks true disciples; John 15 describes His words remaining in the disciples, prayer, and fruit glorifying the Father. The conditional formulations differ.

and you will know the truth, and the truth will set you free.”

  1. Romans 6:17–6:18 thematic parallel

    Both connect the reception of teaching with freedom in a discussion of sin's slavery. John speaks of knowing the truth and being freed; Romans speaks of heartfelt obedience, freedom from sin, and service to righteousness. Neither passage is reduced to a claim about civil status alone.

    Romans 6:18 and, having been set free from sin, you were enslaved to righteousness.

  1. Genesis 15:13 thematic parallel

    Both concern Abraham's offspring and servitude. Genesis predicts descendants' oppression and enslavement in a foreign land; John's hearers assert that they have never been slaves. The comparison does not settle whether their claim concerns personal status or ancestral history.

John 8:34

Jesus answered them, “Amen, amen, I tell you: everyone who commits sin is a slave of sin.

  1. Romans 6:16–6:18 thematic parallel

    Both depict sin as a form of slavery. Jesus speaks of the person committing sin as sin's slave; Paul discusses obedience, sin leading to death, and transfer into service of righteousness. Their arguments are related without being identical accounts of human bondage.

    Romans 6:17 But thanks be to God that, though you were slaves of sin, you obeyed from the heart the pattern of teaching to which you were handed over;

    Hebrews 3:6 but Christ as a Son over His house. We are that house if indeed we hold fast our confidence and the boast of our hope.

    Both attribute freedom to the Son or Christ. John speaks of being truly free after describing sin's slavery; Galatians urges standing in Christ's freedom rather than a yoke of slavery in its own argument. John's saying is not rewritten as the whole Galatian controversy.

    John 8:40 But now you seek to kill Me, a man who has spoken to you the truth that I heard from God. Abraham did not do this.

John 8:44

You are from your father, the devil, and you want to carry out your father’s desires. He was a murderer from the beginning and has not stood in the truth, because there is no truth in him. When he speaks the lie, he speaks from what is his own, because he is a liar and its father.

    Both describe the devil in relation to the beginning and moral parentage; the Epistle also describes Cain's murderous conduct as from the evil one. John 8 explicitly adds lying and a murderer from the beginning. This is a moral-character comparison, not a hereditary judgment on an ethnicity.

    Both distinguish glory connected with God from human honor. John 8 says Jesus does not seek His own glory and another seeks and judges; John 5 says He does not receive human glory and challenges mutual human praise. Seeking and receiving are preserved as different expressions.

John 8:58

  1. John 1:1–1:4 thematic parallel

    Jesus contrasts Abraham's coming into being with His own 'I am'; the prologue describes the Word already being in the beginning and creation coming into being through Him. This is an internal preexistence comparison, not a substitution of the prologue's wording for Jesus' exact saying.

    Read matching passage

    John 1:4 in Him was life, and the life was the light of humankind.

  2. Exodus 3:14 allusion

    Jesus' absolute 'I am' can be compared with God's self-designation to Moses. Exodus gives a fuller designation and Moses' commission. This is a possible divine-name allusion, not an assertion that the two passages contain an identical complete English formula.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 3:14 And God said to Moses, “I am the One Who Is.” And He said, “This is what you shall say to the sons of Israel: ‘The One Who Is has sent me to you.’”

    Both describe opponents taking up stones against Jesus. John 10 explicitly supplies a blasphemy accusation; John 8 follows the Abraham exchange and describes Jesus hiding and leaving. John 10's spoken accusation is not imported as a quotation into John 8.

    Mark 8:25 Then He laid His hands on his eyes again. The man saw clearly and was restored, and could see everything distinctly.

And He said to him, “Go, wash in the pool of Siloam”—which is translated Sent. So he went away and washed, and came back seeing.

  1. 2 Kings 5:10–5:14 narrative parallel

    Both healing narratives involve obedience to a directive to wash. John names Siloam, translated Sent, and restoration of sight; Elisha directs Naaman to wash seven times in the Jordan for his skin condition. John's man is not Naaman and its procedure is not sevenfold.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 5:10 Elisha sent a messenger to him, saying, ‘Go and bathe seven times in the Jordan. Your flesh will be restored to you, and you will be cleansed.’

    2 Kings 5:11 Naaman became angry and went away, saying, ‘Behold, I thought that he would come out to me, stand, call on the name of his God, place his hand on the place, and remove the leprous condition.’

    2 Kings 5:12 ‘Are not Abanah and Pharpar, the rivers of Damascus, better than the Jordan and all the waters of Israel? Could I not go and bathe in them and be cleansed?’ He turned aside and went away in anger.

    2 Kings 5:13 His attendants approached and spoke to him: ‘The prophet has told you a great thing; will you not do it? And he has said to you, Bathe and be cleansed.’

    2 Kings 5:14 And Naaman went down, and dipped himself in the Jordan seven times, according to the word of Elisha; and his flesh was restored as the flesh of a little child, and he was cleansed.

    Both describe public recognition of a formerly disabled beggar after healing. John concerns a man formerly blind; Acts concerns the man who had sat at the Beautiful Gate and now walks praising God. The healers and disabilities differ.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 3:9 All the people saw him walking and praising God.

    Acts 3:10 They recognized him as the one who used to sit for alms at the temple’s Beautiful Gate, and they were filled with wonder and astonishment at what had happened to him.

John 9:9

Some said, “This is he.” Others said, “No, but he is like him.” He said, “I am he.”

  1. Acts 3:9–3:10 narrative parallel

    Both healed beggars become objects of public identity recognition. John reports disagreement and the man's own identification; Acts says the people recognized the former alms-seeker now walking and praising God. The disabilities, responses and individuals differ, and the ordinary I am he is not equated with Jesus' distinct identity sayings.

  1. John 9:15 thematic parallel

    The neighbors' question about how the eyes opened recurs when the Pharisees ask how the man received sight. The narrator explicitly says they asked again. This is an internal recurrence within one investigation, not a second healing or independent attestation.

    The man's account of Jesus making mud, applying it, directing him to Siloam, and his washing and seeing recalls the narrated actions. It is the character's report within this same account, not an independently preserved second record.

    Both healed men are questioned about Jesus, with different limits of knowledge. John5 says the healed man did not know who it was; John9 has already named Jesus but does not know where He is. This sharpens the reported who/where difference without inventing broader ignorance, lack of faith or an escape method.

    Both contrast the flock's good with exploitative leadership. John 10:15

just as the Father knows Me and I know the Father; and I lay down My life for the sheep.

  1. Matthew 11:27 thematic parallel

    Both affirm mutual Father-Son knowledge. John uses it in relation to shepherd-and-sheep knowledge and Jesus' self-giving; Matthew also speaks of the Son revealing the Father. Neither is a verbatim repetition of the other's full argument.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 11:27 All things have been handed over to Me by My Father. No one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and anyone to whom the Son wishes to reveal Him.

    Pinned target record

John 10:16

And I have other sheep that are not from this fold. I must bring them also, and they will hear My voice, and they will become one flock, one shepherd.

  1. Ezekiel 37:24 thematic parallel

    Both use one-shepherd language. Ezekiel names David as ruler and speaks of one shepherd over all in Israel's restoration setting; John promises other sheep hearing Jesus and one flock, one shepherd. John says flock rather than fold here, and this comparison does not itself identify every group of sheep.

    Read matching passage

    Ezekiel 37:24 My servant David will be ruler among them, and one shepherd will be over all, because they will walk in My ordinances, keep My judgments and do them.

    Pinned target record

John 10:17

For this reason the Father loves Me, because I lay down My life, so that I may take it again.

  1. John 2:19–2:22 thematic parallel

    Both relate Jesus to the raising of His own body or the taking of His life again. John 2 interprets the sanctuary saying as about His body and later resurrection; John 10 speaks of laying down life, taking it again, and the Father's love. Their images differ.

    Read matching passage

    John 2:19 Jesus answered them, “Destroy this sanctuary, and in three days I will raise it.”

    John 2:20 So the Jews said, “This sanctuary was built in forty-six years, and wilt Thou raise it in three days?”

    John 2:21 But He was speaking about the sanctuary of His body.

    John 2:22 When, therefore, He was raised from the dead, His disciples remembered that He had said this, and they believed the Scripture and the word Jesus had spoken.

John 10:18

No one takes it from Me, but I lay it down of My own accord. I have authority to lay it down, and I have authority to take it again. This command I received from My Father.”

  1. John 19:10–19:11 thematic parallel

    Both discuss authority concerning Jesus' death. John 10 states His own authority to lay down and take His life under the Father's command; John 19 makes Pilate's claimed authority dependent on a grant from above. Voluntary self-giving does not erase the human agency reported in the Passion.

    Read matching passage

    John 19:10 Then Pilate says to Him, “Dost Thou not speak to me? Dost Thou not know that I have authority to release Thee, and I have authority to crucify Thee?”

    John 19:11 Jesus answered him, “You would have no authority against Me unless it had been given you from above. For this reason the one who handed Me over to you has greater sin.”

John 10:19

  1. John 9:16 thematic parallel

    The renewed division recalls the earlier division over the Sabbath healing and whether Jesus could be from God. John 10 concerns His present words and ensuing demon dispute. This is the Gospel's recurring divided response, not two independent accounts of one conversation.

    Read matching passage

    John 9:16 Then some of the Pharisees said, “This man is not from God, because He does not keep the Sabbath.” But others said, “How can a sinful man do such signs?” And there was division among them.

John 10:20

Many of them said, “He has a demon and is mad. Why do you listen to Him?”

  1. John 7:20 thematic parallel

    Both report an accusation that Jesus has a demon. John 10 adds madness and a question about listening; John 7's crowd asks who seeks to kill Him. These are hostile speakers' claims, not narrator affirmations.

    John 7:20 The crowd answered, “Thou hast a demon! Who seeks to kill Thee?”

John 10:21

Others said, “These are not the words of a demon-possessed person. Can a demon open the eyes of the blind?”

  1. John 9:6–9:7 thematic parallel

    The question about opening blind eyes recalls the earlier narrated healing with mud and washing. The later speakers use such a work against the demon accusation. Their rhetorical question is not an independently preserved account of a second healing.

    John 9:6 Having said these things, He spat on the ground and made mud from the spittle, and spread the mud upon his eyes.

John 10:22

Then the feast of Dedication took place in Jerusalem. It was winter,

  1. 1 Maccabees 4:52–4:59 thematic parallel

    First Maccabees narrates the altar's rededication and the annual eight-day observance beginning on the twenty-fifth of the ninth month. This supplies background for John's feast of Dedication. It does not narrate Jesus' visit or fix its year from the Maccabean date.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 4:52 They rose early in the morning on the twenty-fifth day of the ninth month, which is the month of Chislev, in the one hundred and forty-eighth year.

    1 Maccabees 4:53 They offered sacrifice according to the law upon the new altar of whole burnt offerings that they had made.

    1 Maccabees 4:54 At the season and on the day on which the nations had profaned it, it was dedicated with songs, harps, lyres, and cymbals.

    1 Maccabees 4:55 All the people fell face down, worshiped, and blessed toward heaven the One who had prospered them.

    1 Maccabees 4:56 They celebrated the dedication of the altar for eight days, offered whole burnt offerings with gladness, and sacrificed a sacrifice of well-being and praise.

    1 Maccabees 4:57 They adorned the front of the temple with golden crowns and small shields, renewed the gateways and chambers, and fitted them with doors.

    1 Maccabees 4:58 There was exceedingly great gladness among the people, and the reproach of the nations was turned away.

    1 Maccabees 4:59 Judas, his brothers, and the whole assembly of Israel established that the days of the altar’s dedication should be celebrated at their proper season, year by year, for eight days beginning on the twenty-fifth of the month of Chislev, with gladness and joy.

    Both describe Jesus' works as testimony about Him in relation to the Father. John 10 speaks of works done in the Father's name; John 5 speaks of works given to complete and testifying that the Father sent Him. The giving and name-language are preserved separately.

    Both explain lack of reception through relationship language. John 8 speaks of not hearing God's words because one is not from God; John 10 speaks of unbelief because the hearers are not His sheep. The comparison does not supply a separate account of how one becomes a sheep.

    Both connect eternal life and the preservation of those given by the Father. John 6 speaks of losing none and last-day raising; John 10 speaks of never perishing and no snatching from Jesus' hand. The preservation images are related without replacing their particular words.

  1. John 17:21–17:23 thematic parallel

    Both use Father-Son oneness language. John 10 briefly says 'I and the Father are one'; John 17 describes reciprocal indwelling and prays for believers' unity. The related language is not made an exhaustive definition of every sense of 'one' in the earlier saying.

John 10:38

  1. John 14:10–14:11 thematic parallel

    Both connect the works with Father-Son indwelling, but John 10 urges belief in the works so the hearers may know and continue to know that indwelling; John 14 urges belief in the indwelling or because of the works. John 14 also distinguishes Jesus' speech from the Father's indwelling works. This is a close internal recurrence of reciprocal language, not independent historical attestation.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:10 Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me? The words that I say to you I do not speak from Myself; but the Father, who remains in Me, does His works.

John 11:19

Many of the Jews had come to Martha and Mary to console them concerning their brother.

  1. John 11:31 internal consolation-group continuation

    Both describe the people consoling Mary and Martha over their brother. The later verse follows those with Mary as she leaves; it does not independently attest every earlier arrival or number.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:31 So the Jews who were with her in the house consoling her, seeing that Mary rose quickly and went out, followed her, thinking that she was going to the tomb to weep there.

John 11:20

When Martha heard that Jesus was coming, she went to meet Him, but Mary remained seated in the house.

  1. John 11:29 internal sister-response comparison

    Martha hears Jesus is coming and goes to meet Him; Mary later hears the summons and rises quickly to come. Their responses occur at different moments, and Mary's initial staying home is not erased.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:29 When she heard, she rose quickly and came to Him.

    Pinned target record

John 11:21

So Martha said to Jesus, “Lord, if Thou hadst been here, my brother would not have died.

  1. John 11:32 internal repeated-saying link

    Martha and Mary each tell Jesus that their brother would not have died had He been there. The repeated saying is an explicit literary counterpart; Mary's falling at His feet belongs to her later scene.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:32 When Mary came where Jesus was and saw Him, she fell at His feet and said to Him, “Lord, if Thou hadst been here, my brother would not have died.”

    Pinned target record

John 11:22

But even now I know that whatever Thou askest of God, God will give Thee.”

  1. John 11:41–11:42 internal prayer-confidence link

    Martha expresses confidence that God answers Jesus; Jesus later thanks the Father for hearing Him and says He always hears. Martha's confidence and Jesus' thanksgiving have different speakers and forms, and neither is turned into a newly supplied divine promise or a record of an unreported petition.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:41 So they took away the stone. Jesus lifted His eyes upward and said, “Father, I thank Thee that Thou didst hear Me.

    John 11:42 I knew that Thou always hearest Me, but I said this because of the crowd standing around, so that they may believe that Thou didst send Me.”

    Pinned target record

John 11:23

Jesus said to her, “Your brother will rise.”

  1. John 11:43–11:44 promise and narrated event

    Jesus' promise that the brother will rise is followed by His call to Lazarus and the dead man's emergence. The later account supplies the concrete raising, not Martha's initial interpretation of its timing.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”

    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

    Pinned target record

John 11:24

Martha said to Him, “I know that he will rise in the resurrection on the last day.”

  1. John 6:39–6:40 resurrection-teaching comparison

    Both explicitly speak of raising on the last day. Martha applies that expectation to her brother; Jesus' earlier teaching speaks of those given to Him and those beholding and believing the Son. The individual expectation and broader promises remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:39 And this is the will of the One who sent Me: that of everything He has given Me I should lose nothing, but raise it up on the last day.

    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

    Pinned target record

John 11:25

Jesus said to her, “I am the resurrection and the life; the one who believes in Me will live, even if he dies,

  1. John 6:40 life-and-belief teaching comparison

    Both connect believing in Jesus/the Son with life and resurrection. John11 calls Jesus resurrection and life even across death; John6 promises eternal life and last-day raising. Their exact descriptions and time language are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:40 For this is My Father’s will: that everyone who beholds the Son and believes in Him should have eternal life, and I Myself will raise him up on the last day.”

    Pinned target record

John 11:26

and everyone who lives and believes in Me will certainly never die. Do you believe this?”

  1. John 8:51 life-promise comparison

    Both promise not beholding or undergoing death in relation to Jesus. John11 names living and believing; John8 names keeping His word. The conditions and expressions are not made verbatim or turned into an unsupported claim that every believer avoids bodily death.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:51 Amen, amen, I tell you: if anyone keeps My word, he will certainly never behold death.”

    Pinned target record

John 11:27

She said to Him, “Yes, Lord. I have believed that Thou art the Christ, the Son of God, the One coming into the world.”

  1. Matthew 16:16 confession parallel

    Both confess Jesus as the Christ and God's Son. Martha additionally names the One coming into the world; Peter says Son of the living God. The speakers and occasions are not identified.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 16:16 Simon Peter answered, “Thou art the Christ, the Son of the living God.”

John 11:28

Having said this, she went away and called her sister Mary secretly, saying, “The Teacher is here and calls you.”

  1. Mark 10:49 summons-message comparison

    Both convey to another person that Jesus calls them. Martha secretly tells Mary the Teacher is here and calls; the crowd encourages the blind man that Jesus is calling. Recipients, messengers and circumstances differ.

    Mark 10:49 Jesus stopped and said, “Call him.” They called the blind man, saying to him, “Take courage! Get up; He is calling you.”

When she heard, she rose quickly and came to Him.

    Mary quickly rises and goes after hearing the summons, corresponding to Martha's earlier going to meet Jesus upon hearing His arrival. The two sisters and the different messages remain distinct.

  1. John 11:20 narrative parallel

    The reference to where Martha had met Jesus looks back to her earlier going to meet Him while Mary remained in the house. This distinguishes the two sisters' meetings within the narrative. The earlier unit does not independently measure Jesus' position or repeat the later statement that He had not entered the village.

    The people consoling Mary are the same narrative's previously introduced consolers of the sisters. The earlier notice does not independently report their following her or their mistaken assumption about a tomb visit.

    Mary's conditional statement about Jesus' presence and her brother's death repeats Martha's earlier words. Mary also falls at His feet; that gesture is not assigned to Martha's statement.

    Both describe Jesus being troubled in spirit. The raising scene also names indignation while people weep; the supper scene introduces His betrayal announcement. The causes, occasions and additional emotions are different.

John 11:34

And He said, “Where have you laid him?” They said to Him, “Lord, come and see.”

  1. John 20:13–20:15 narrative parallel

    Both scenes combine weeping with a question about where a body has been laid. Jesus asks about Lazarus and receives a come-and-see answer; Magdalene seeks where Jesus has been laid while not recognizing Him. The speakers and bodies differ, and neither Mary is identified with the other or either question made proof of an unstated limit of divine knowledge.

    Read matching passage

    John 20:14 Having said these things, she turned backward and beholds Jesus standing there, and did not know that it was Jesus.

    John 20:15 Jesus says to her, “Woman, why do you weep? Whom do you seek?” She, supposing that He is the gardener, says to Him, “Sir, if Thou didst carry Him away, tell me where Thou didst lay Him, and I will take Him away.”

John 11:35

    Both explicitly narrate Jesus weeping. John places the tears in Lazarus's mourning scene; Luke places them at the sight of Jerusalem. The objects and circumstances of grief are not equated.

    The onlookers' statement about Jesus loving Lazarus recalls the sisters' description of the ill man as the one He loves. Different speakers interpret different moments; the earlier message does not independently narrate these tears.

John 11:37

But some of them said, “Could not this man, who opened the blind man’s eyes, have kept this man from dying too?”

  1. John 9:6–9:7 internal healing-reference comparison

    The onlookers recall opening a blind man's eyes; the earlier Johannine account narrates Jesus' mud-and-washing healing followed by sight. That account supplies a concrete counterpart to the recalled deed, not the later claim about preventing Lazarus's death or a list of every witness.

    Read matching passage

    John 9:6 Having said these things, He spat on the ground and made mud from the spittle, and spread the mud upon his eyes.

    John 9:7 And He said to him, “Go, wash in the pool of Siloam”—which is translated Sent. So he went away and washed, and came back seeing.

  1. John 20:1 tomb-stone comparison

    Both raising accounts describe a stone at a tomb. Lazarus's cave still has its stone before the removal command; Jesus' tomb is found with its stone already removed. The tombs, deceased persons and narrative stages are different.

    Both prayers connect hearers' belief with the Father's sending Jesus. Here Jesus explains His public words for the crowd; John17 describes disciples receiving His words and believing the sending. The crowd, disciples and reported stages of belief differ.

John 11:43

Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”

  1. John 5:28–5:29 voice-and-life sign comparison

    Jesus' call into Lazarus's tomb provides a specific example to compare with His teaching that those in tombs will hear His voice and come out. One raising does not complete John5's universal future resurrection or its contrasting outcomes.

    Read matching passage

    John 5:29 and come out: those who did good things to a resurrection of life, but those who practiced base things to a resurrection of judgment.

John 11:44

The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

  1. John 20:6–20:7 grave-clothing comparison

    Both accounts describe burial cloths including a face cloth. Lazarus emerges still bound and Jesus tells those present to unbind and release him; Peter finds Jesus' linen lying and face cloth separately wrapped. The addressees, states of the cloths and raising narratives are not made identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 20:7 and the face cloth that had been on His head, not lying with the linen cloths, but wrapped up separately in one place.

John 11:45

So many of the Jews who had come to Mary and beheld what He did believed in Him.

  1. John 2:23 sign-and-belief narrative comparison

    Both narrate many people believing after seeing Jesus' signs or deed. John11 concerns witnesses with Mary after Lazarus's raising; John2 concerns Jerusalem's feast signs. Neither account independently counts the other's converts or establishes identical quality of belief.

John 11:46

But some of them went away to the Pharisees and told them what Jesus did.

  1. John 5:15–5:16 report-and-opposition comparison

    Both narrate people reporting a healing deed to religious interlocutors before opposition intensifies. John11 has some witnesses inform Pharisees; John5 has the restored man identify Jesus to the Jews. The reporters, methods and unspoken motives are not equated.

John 11:47

Then the chief priests and the Pharisees gathered a council and said, “What are we doing? For this man does many signs.

  1. Matthew 26:3–26:4 council-scene comparison

    Both describe religious leaders gathering before a death plot against Jesus. Matthew names the high priest Caiaphas and chief priests/elders; John names chief priests/Pharisees and their acknowledgment of many signs. Their meetings, speeches and stages are not declared identical.

    Matthew 26:4 They took counsel to seize Jesus by deceit and kill Him.

John 11:48

If we leave Him like this, everyone will believe in Him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and our nation.”

  1. John 19:12–19:15 thematic parallel

    The council invokes the Romans as a threat to their place and nation if belief spreads; the Passion scene invokes Caesar while pressuring Pilate, and the chief priests profess Caesar as king. This compares explicit Roman political arguments around Jesus without verifying the council's prediction, defining place as one compulsory referent or equating every participant.

    Read matching passage

    John 19:13 So Pilate, hearing these words, brought Jesus outside and sat on the judgment seat at a place called Stone Pavement, and in Hebrew, Gabbatha.

    John 19:14 Now it was the Preparation of the Passover; it was about the sixth hour. And he says to the Jews, “Behold your King!”

    John 19:15 Then they cried out, “Take Him away! Take Him away! Crucify Him!” Pilate says to them, “Shall I crucify your King?” The chief priests answered, “We have no king except Caesar.”

  1. John 11:50 internal speech-and-interpretation link

    The narrator interprets Caiaphas's preceding one-for-the-people advice as prophecy of Jesus' death for the nation. The hostile counselor's words and the narrator's explanation are distinguished; no unreported understanding of the prophecy is assigned to Caiaphas.

John 12:12

The next day, the great crowd that had come to the feast, hearing that Jesus was coming to Jerusalem,

  1. Mark 11:8–11:10 entry-scene comparison

    Both place a public crowd reception at Jesus' Jerusalem approach. John introduces a feast crowd hearing of His arrival before the next unit's acclamation; Mark describes garments, branches and those ahead/behind acclaiming Him. The next-day and feast-participant details are not supplied from Mark.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 11:8 Many spread their garments on the road; others spread leafy branches they had cut from the fields.

    Mark 11:9 Those going ahead and those following cried out, “Hosanna! Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord!

    Mark 11:10 Blessed is the coming kingdom of our father David! Hosanna in the highest!”

    Pinned target record

John 12:13

took branches of the palm trees and went out to meet Him, and cried out, “Hosanna! Blessed is the One who comes in the name of the Lord, even the King of Israel!”

  1. Psalms 117:26 quotation

    The crowd's blessing of the One coming in the Lord's name corresponds to the psalm's blessing. John adds Hosanna, King of Israel and palm branches in the entry narrative; the psalm adds blessing from the Lord's house.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 117:26 Blessed is the one who comes in the name of the Lord. We have blessed you from the house of the Lord.

Jesus found a young donkey and sat upon it, just as it is written,

  1. Mark 11:7 entry-scene parallel

    Both describe Jesus seated on a colt. John says He found a young donkey and introduces Scripture; Mark says disciples brought the colt and put garments on it. The means of obtaining it are not merged.

    Mark 11:7 They brought the colt to Jesus, threw their garments on it, and He sat on it.

    Both announce Zion's coming king mounted on the animal. John says Do not fear and a donkey's colt; Zechariah says Rejoice/proclaim and a beast of burden and young colt, adding righteous, saving and gentle. The actual supplied animal and introductory wording remain distinct.

  1. John 2:22 later-remembrance comparison

    Both recount disciples later remembering Scripture or Jesus' words. John12 says after His glorification concerning entry actions; John2 says after His resurrection concerning the temple saying. The remembered content and time formulations are not made identical.

John 12:17

  1. John 11:43–11:44 internal eyewitness-and-event link

    The entry narrative explicitly recalls the crowd present when Jesus called Lazarus from the tomb. The earlier passage narrates that call and emergence; it does not independently narrate the later testimony or identify every witness.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:43 Having said these things, He cried out with a loud voice, “Lazarus, come out!”

    John 11:44 The dead man came out, his feet and hands bound with strips of cloth, and his face wrapped with a cloth. Jesus said to them, “Unbind him, and let him go.”

John 12:18

For this reason the crowd also met Him, because they heard that He had done this sign.

  1. John 12:17 internal testimony-and-reception link

    The crowd's meeting Jesus because it heard of the sign follows the earlier eyewitness crowd's testimony about Lazarus. The narrative connects the same sign with testimony and reception; eyewitnesses and hearers are not automatically one group.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:17 So the crowd that was with Him when He called Lazarus from the tomb and raised him from the dead bore witness.

John 12:19

  1. John 11:48 internal opponents-response comparison

    Both report authorities treating widespread belief in Jesus as their problem. John12's Pharisees say the world went after Him; John11's speakers fear everyone believing and Roman action. Their rhetoric is not an independently verified worldwide conversion count.

    Read matching passage

    John 11:48 If we leave Him like this, everyone will believe in Him, and the Romans will come and take away both our place and our nation.”

  1. Isaiah 56:6–56:7 scriptural worship-background comparison

    Greeks going up to worship can be compared with Isaiah's foreigners attached to the Lord and welcome in His prayer house for all nations. John does not specify these individuals' circumcision, Sabbath or covenant status, or declare this group complete fulfillment of the promise.

    Read matching passage

    Both identify Philip with Bethsaida in Galilee's ministry context. John12 additionally records Greeks' request to see Jesus; John1 describes Philip's call and the town shared with Andrew and Peter, without independently narrating that request.

  1. John 1:40–1:46 narrative parallel

    Andrew and Philip mediate the request to Jesus here; earlier Andrew brings Simon and Philip tells Nathanael about Jesus and invites him to see. The named disciples' mediating roles recur, but the persons and actions differ. The comparison does not assert that the Greeks' requested audience occurs or supply the motive for Philip consulting Andrew.

    Both announce the Son of Man's glorification. John12 says the hour has come; John13 says now after Judas leaves and names God's reciprocal glorification. Their temporal markers and explanatory clauses are preserved.

John 12:24

  1. 1 Corinthians 15:36–15:38 grain-and-life imagery comparison

    Both use a sown grain's death before subsequent life or fruit. Jesus speaks of wheat no longer remaining alone but bearing much fruit; Paul discusses the resurrection body's relation to bare seed and God's giving a body. The images serve different expositions, not one literal botanical or resurrection timetable.

    Read matching passage

    1 Corinthians 15:36 Foolish one! What you sow is not made alive unless it dies.

    1 Corinthians 15:38 But God gives it a body just as He wished, and to each of the seeds its own body.

  1. Mark 8:35 life-loss teaching comparison

    Both contrast preserving life by surrender with losing it through attachment to present life. John says loving/hating one's life in this world and keeping it for eternal life; Mark names losing it for Jesus and the gospel. The supplied verbs and qualifications remain distinct.

    Both portray Jesus deeply troubled while facing His hour. Father, glorify Thy name.” Then a voice came from heaven: “I both glorified it and will glorify it again.”

  1. Psalms 28:3 thematic parallel

    The Psalm explicitly joins the Lord's voice with the God of glory thundering. John's crowd hears the heavenly voice but divides between thunder and an angel speaking. This compares concrete divine-voice/thunder imagery without endorsing both crowd explanations as fact, inventing a storm or making the Psalm a report of this event.

    The crowd's Son-of-Man question and expectation of lasting messianic rule can be compared with Daniel's son-of-man figure and everlasting authority/kingdom.     Both connect available light with safe walking and darkness with impaired movement. John12 names the Light's short remaining presence and not knowing one's direction; John11 names day, night and stumbling. Their images and time descriptions are not identical.

    Both concern eyes, heart, turning and healing. John says He blinded and hardened; the supplied Isaiah says the people's heart grew dull, ears heard heavily and they closed their eyes. The agency, hearing clause and sentence forms are preserved rather than harmonized.

    Isaiah narrates seeing the enthroned Lord and speaks of His glory filling house and earth. John applies Isaiah's seeing and speaking to Jesus' glory. The identification is John's stated application; Isaiah's vision is not rewritten to contain Jesus' name or a new independent identification.

    Peter's question about the Lord washing his feet can be compared with Jesus' later Lord-and-Teacher explanation that He washed their feet as a pattern for mutual service. The later teaching explains the role reversal, not independent detail of Peter's words.

    Read matching passage

John 13:7

    Jesus contrasts Peter's present nonunderstanding with promised later understanding; John12 contrasts initial nonunderstanding with later remembering of Scripture and entry actions after glorification.   1. 1 Corinthians 6:11 washing-and-participation comparison

    Both connect washing with a changed holy relationship. John makes Jesus' washing necessary for Peter's share with Him; Corinthians says you washed yourselves clean, then were sanctified and justified in Jesus' name and God's Spirit. Different agency and language are preserved without equating foot washing and a particular rite.
