John 13:9

Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, not my feet only, but also my hands and my head!”

  1. Exodus 30:18–30:21 thematic parallel

    Peter requests hands and head as well as feet after Jesus' no-share warning. Exodus specifies priestly hand-and-foot washing before service. Both specify bodily washing in relation to participation, but Exodus does not add head washing here, and John does not make Peter an Aaronite or impose the priestly death warning on this conversation.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 30:18 “Make a bronze basin with a bronze base for washing. Put it between the Tent of Testimony and the altar, and pour water into it.

    Exodus 30:19 Aaron and his sons shall wash their hands and feet with water from it.

    Exodus 30:20 When they enter the Tent of Testimony, they shall wash with water so that they do not die. When they approach the altar to minister and offer whole burnt offerings to the Lord,

    Exodus 30:21 they shall wash their hands and feet with water. When they enter the Tent of Testimony, they shall wash with water so that they do not die. This shall be a perpetual statute for him and his generations after him.”

    Pinned target record

John 13:10

Jesus said to him, “The one who has bathed has no need to wash except the feet, but is wholly clean. And you are clean, but not all.”

  1. John 15:3 disciples-cleanness comparison

    Both call Jesus' disciples clean. John13 uses bathing/feet language and an exception explained through the betrayer; John15 attributes cleanness to the spoken word without repeating that exception. The grounds and qualifications remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 15:3 You are already clean because of the word that I have spoken to you.

    Pinned target record

John 13:11

For He knew the one who was handing Him over; for this reason He said, “Not all of you are clean.”

  1. John 6:64 betrayal-foreknowledge comparison

    Both explicitly describe Jesus knowing the one who would hand Him over. John13 uses that knowledge to explain not-all-clean; John6 describes knowledge from the beginning alongside unbelief. No new account of compulsion or unidentified persons is supplied.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:64 But there are some among you who do not believe.” For Jesus knew from the beginning who those were who did not believe and who it was who would hand Him over.

    Pinned target record

John 13:12

So when He had washed their feet and taken His garments and reclined again, He said to them, “Do you understand what I have done to you?

  1. John 13:15 internal act-and-example explanation

    Jesus' question about understanding what He did receives its stated example-purpose in13:15. The link interprets the narrated foot washing through His own teaching, not another independent washing account.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:15 For I have given you an example, so that you also may do just as I did to you.

    Pinned target record

John 13:13

You call Me the Teacher and the Lord, and you speak well, for I am.

  1. Luke 6:46 Lord-title and obedience comparison

    Both relate calling Jesus Lord to the response He expects. John affirms Teacher and Lord before His service command; Luke challenges Lord, Lord speech without doing His sayings. The title affirmation and rebuke have different settings and wording.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 6:46 Why do you call Me ‘Lord, Lord,’ and not do what I say?

    Pinned target record

John 13:14

So if I, the Lord and the Teacher, washed your feet, you also ought to wash one another’s feet.

  1. 1 Timothy 5:10 command-and-practice comparison

    Jesus commands mutual foot washing after His example; Timothy names washing saints' feet as a good work. The later description offers a concrete service counterpart, not proof that every recipient reenacted this supper or that the directive had one unstated institutional form.

    Read matching passage

    1 Timothy 5:10 attested for good works: if she brought up children, if she received strangers, if she washed saints’ feet, if she assisted the afflicted, if she followed every good work.

    Pinned target record

John 13:15

For I have given you an example, so that you also may do just as I did to you.

  1. 1 Peter 2:21 example-and-imitation comparison

    Both describe Christ's action as an example for followers. John concerns mutual service following foot washing; Peter concerns suffering and following His footsteps. Different acts and contexts are not conflated.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 2:21 For to this you were called, because Christ also suffered for you, leaving you an example, so that you might follow in His footsteps.

    Pinned target record

John 13:16

Amen, amen, I tell you: a slave is not greater than his master, nor a messenger greater than the one who sent him.

  1. Matthew 10:24–10:25 teaching parallel

    Both deny a slave being greater than the master. John also names messenger and sender in the service setting; Matthew names disciple/teacher and applies the pattern to hostile treatment. The contexts and additional analogies differ.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 10:24 A disciple is not above the teacher, nor a slave above his master.

    Matthew 10:25 It is enough for the disciple to become like his teacher, and the slave like his master. If they called the master of the house Beelzebul, how much more his household members!

    Pinned target record

John 13:17

If you know these things, blessed are you if you do them.

  1. Luke 11:28 knowing-and-doing blessing comparison

    Both connect blessing with acting on received teaching rather than reception alone. John names knowing and doing these things; Luke names hearing God's word and keeping it. The instruction contexts and formulations remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 11:28 But He said, “Rather, blessed are those who hear the word of God and keep it!”

    Pinned target record

John 13:18

I do not speak about all of you. I know whom I chose. But so that the Scripture may be fulfilled: ‘The one eating My bread lifted his heel against Me.’

  1. Psalms 40:10 quotation comparison

    Both describe betrayal by a trusted table companion. John says eating My bread and lifting a heel; the actual psalm says eating my loaves and magnifying treachery. The heel image is not inserted into this supplied psalm, and Judas is not named there.

    Read matching passage

    Psalms 40:10 For even the man of my peace, in whom I hoped, who ate my loaves, magnified treachery against me.

    Pinned target record

John 13:19

From now on I tell you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe that I am.

  1. John 14:29 forewarning-and-belief recurrence

    Both say Jesus tells the disciples before an event so that they believe when it occurs. John13 additionally says that I am; John14 does not repeat that object in the cited unit.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:29 And now I have told you before it happens, so that when it happens you may believe.

    Pinned target record

John 13:20

Amen, amen, I tell you: the one who receives anyone I send receives Me, and the one who receives Me receives the One who sent Me.”

  1. Matthew 10:40 messenger-reception parallel

    Both link receiving Jesus' messengers with receiving Jesus and the One who sent Him. John says anyone I send; Matthew addresses you. The chain is explicit without identifying every later messenger.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 10:40 Whoever receives you receives Me, and whoever receives Me receives the One who sent Me.

    Pinned target record

John 13:21

Having said these things, Jesus was troubled in spirit and testified, saying, “Amen, amen, I tell you that one of you will hand Me over.”

  1. Mark 14:18 betrayal-announcement parallel

    Both announce that one of the supper group will hand Jesus over. John describes His troubled spirit; Mark names someone eating with Him. The emotions, wording and feast chronology are not merged.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 14:18 As they reclined and ate, Jesus said, “Truly I tell you, one of you will hand Me over—one who is eating with Me.”

    Pinned target record

John 13:22

The disciples looked at one another, perplexed about whom He was speaking.

  1. Mark 14:19 betrayal-response comparison

    Both describe the group's disturbed uncertainty about the betrayer. John narrates exchanged looks and perplexity; Mark narrates grief and individual Surely-not-I questions. The responses are not made verbally identical.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 14:19 They began to be grieved and said to Him one by one, “Surely not I?”

    Pinned target record

John 13:23

One of His disciples, whom Jesus loved, was reclining in Jesus’ bosom.

  1. John 21:20 internal disciple-description callback

    John21 explicitly recalls the loved disciple reclining against Jesus at supper and asking about betrayal. This identifies the narrative description repeated here without assigning the unnamed disciple a name not given in these units.

    Read matching passage

    John 21:20 Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

    Pinned target record

John 13:24

So Simon Peter signaled to this one to ask whom He was speaking about.

  1. John 21:20 internal disciple-and-question callback

    The later retrospect identifies the loved supper disciple who asked about betrayal, providing a concrete counterpart for the unnamed one Peter prompts here. It does not independently narrate Peter's signal or supply a personal name for the narrative's unnamed description.

    Read matching passage

    John 21:20 Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

    Pinned target record

John 13:25

Leaning back thus upon Jesus’ chest, he said to Him, “Lord, who is it?”

  1. John 21:20 internal supper-question callback

    John21 explicitly recalls the chest-reclining disciple's Lord-directed betrayal question. John13 says Lord, who is it; John21 expands the question to who hands Thee over. The retrospect's wording and unidentified person remain as supplied.

    Read matching passage

    John 21:20 Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

    Pinned target record

John 13:26

Jesus answered, “It is the one for whom I will dip the morsel and give it to him.” So He dipped the morsel, took it, and gave it to Judas, son of Simon Iscariot.

  1. Mark 14:20 food-and-betrayal comparison

    Both identify the betrayer through shared food. John has Jesus dip and give a morsel to named Judas; Mark names one dipping with Him into the dish. The actions are not made identical or the recipient named within Mark's cited saying.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 14:20 He said to them, “One of the Twelve, the one dipping with Me into the dish.

    Pinned target record

John 13:27

And after the morsel, then Satan entered him. So Jesus said to him, “What you do, do more quickly.”

  1. Luke 22:3 betrayal-agency comparison

    Both explicitly say Satan entered Judas. John places it after the morsel and adds Jesus' do-more-quickly command; Luke places it before the authorities' bargain. The different timing is not harmonized.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 22:3 Then Satan entered Judas, who was called Iscariot and was one of the number of the Twelve.

    Pinned target record

John 13:28

But none of those reclining knew for what purpose He said this to him.

  1. John 13:29 internal ignorance-and-guess explanation

    The group's not knowing why Jesus spoke to Judas is followed by their purchase-or-charity guesses. The next unit explains possible interpretations without establishing that either was Jesus' real purpose.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:29 For some thought, since Judas had the money box, that Jesus was telling him, “Buy what we need for the feast,” or that he should give something to the poor.

    Pinned target record

John 13:29

For some thought, since Judas had the money box, that Jesus was telling him, “Buy what we need for the feast,” or that he should give something to the poor.

  1. John 12:6 internal fiscal-role callback

    The disciples' guesses depend on Judas having the money box, the same role the narrator earlier describes. John12's theft and motive judgment is not transferred into the disciples' later thoughts or made evidence that their charity guess was true.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:6 He said this not because he cared about the poor, but because he was a thief, and having the money box, he used to carry off what was put into it.

    Pinned target record

John 13:30

So, having received the morsel, he went out at once. And it was night.

  1. John 13:26–13:27 internal action-sequence link

    Judas's receiving the morsel and immediate exit follow the narrated gift and do-more-quickly command. The link identifies the sequence's stated antecedents, not independent evidence for the night notice or an unquoted mission.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:26 Jesus answered, “It is the one for whom I will dip the morsel and give it to him.” So He dipped the morsel, took it, and gave it to Judas, son of Simon Iscariot.

    John 13:27 And after the morsel, then Satan entered him. So Jesus said to him, “What you do, do more quickly.”

    Pinned target record

John 13:31

When he had gone out, Jesus said, “Now the Son of Man was glorified, and God was glorified in Him.

  1. John 12:23 glorification-announcement recurrence

    Both announce the Son of Man's glorification. John13 says now after Judas leaves and names God glorified in Him; John12 names the arriving hour. Their stage and wording are not collapsed.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:23 Jesus answered them, saying, “The hour has come for the Son of Man to be glorified.

    Pinned target record

John 13:32

If God was glorified in Him, God will also glorify Him in Himself, and will glorify Him at once.

  1. John 17:1 reciprocal-glory comparison

    Both connect the Son's and Father's glorification. John13 speaks of God glorifying Him in Himself at once; John17 requests the Son's glory so that He may glorify the Father. The condition, request and temporal forms are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    John 17:1 Jesus spoke these things, and, lifting His eyes to heaven, said, “Father, the hour has come. Glorify Thy Son, so that the Son may glorify Thee,

    Pinned target record

John 13:33

Little children, I am with you a little longer. You will seek Me, and just as I told the Jews, ‘Where I go, you cannot come,’ I now tell you also.

  1. John 7:33–7:34 explicit earlier-saying callback

    Jesus expressly recalls the where-I-go-you-cannot-come saying previously addressed to the Jews. John7 gives the earlier little-time and seek/not-find language; John13 now addresses disciples. Audience and where-I-am/go wording are not silently made identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 7:33 So Jesus said, “I am with you for a little time longer, and I go to the One who sent Me.

    John 7:34 You will seek Me and will not find Me, and where I am, you cannot come.”

    Pinned target record

John 13:34

I give you a new commandment: that you love one another. Just as I have loved you, you also are to love one another.

  1. John 15:12 commandment recurrence

    Both command loving one another after Jesus' love. John13 calls it new; John15 states This is My commandment. No additional historical theory of the word new is imposed.

    Read matching passage

    John 15:12 “This is My commandment: that you love one another just as I loved you.

    Pinned target record

John 13:35

By this everyone will know that you are My disciples, if you have love among one another.”

  1. 1 John 3:14 love-as-evidence comparison

    Both present mutual love as evidence of relationship to Jesus or spiritual life. John13 concerns everyone recognizing His disciples; the letter concerns knowing passage from death into life through brotherly love. Observers, claims and warnings differ.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 3:14 We know that we have passed from death into life, because we love the brothers. The one who does not love abides in death.

    Pinned target record

John 13:36

Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”

  1. John 21:18–21:19 later-following comparison

    Jesus' promise that Peter will follow later can be compared with His later Follow Me command after a prediction of Peter's death. John21's narrator interprets that prediction as death glorifying God; it does not settle every meaning or exact execution method of the earlier promise.

    Read matching passage

    John 21:18 Amen, amen, I say to you: when you were younger, you tied your belt around yourself and walked where you wished; but when you grow old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will tie your belt around you and carry you where you do not wish.”

    John 21:19 Now He said this to indicate by what kind of death he would glorify God. And having said this, He says to him, “Follow Me.”

    Pinned target record

John 13:37

Peter said to Him, “Lord, why can I not follow Thee now? I will lay down my life for Thee.”

  1. Mark 14:31 Peter-pledge comparison

    Both give Peter's expressed readiness for death, but John says he will lay down his life for Jesus, while Mark says even if he must die with Him he will not deny Him. In John, Peter also asks why he cannot follow now; Mark places the insistence after the denial prediction. The relation words and speech sequence remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 14:31 He spoke all the more insistently, “Even if I must die with Thee, I will certainly not deny Thee.” They all said the same.

    Pinned target record

John 13:38

Jesus answered, “Will you lay down your life for Me? Amen, amen, I tell you: a rooster will certainly not crow until you deny Me three times.”

  1. Mark 14:30 denial-prediction parallel

    Both predict three denials before cockcrow. John first questions Peter's life-laying pledge and does not say twice; Mark specifies this night and two crows. No crow-count or timing reconciliation is invented.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 14:30 Jesus said to him, “Truly I tell you, today, this very night, before a rooster crows twice, you will deny Me three times.”

    Pinned target record

John 14:1

“Do not let your heart be troubled. Believe in God, and believe also in Me.

  1. John 14:27 internal reassurance recurrence

    Both tell the disciples not to let their hearts be troubled. The opening joins belief in God and Jesus; the later assurance gives Jesus' peace and forbids fear. The repeated comfort does not make every clause identical.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:27 Peace I leave with you; My peace I give you. I do not give to you as the world gives. Do not let your heart be troubled or afraid.

    Pinned target record

John 14:2

In My Father’s house are many dwelling places. If it were not so, would I have told you that I am going to prepare a place for you?

  1. Hebrews 11:16 prepared-home comparison

    Both describe a divinely prepared future home. Jesus speaks of His Father's house and preparing dwelling places; Hebrews names God preparing a city for those desiring a heavenly country. The metaphors, recipients and descriptions of preparation are not converted into an architectural map.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 11:16 But now they desire a better one, that is, a heavenly one. Therefore God is not ashamed of them, to be called their God, for He has prepared a city for them.

    Pinned target record

John 14:3

And if I go and prepare a place for you, I am coming again and will take you to Myself, so that where I am, you also may be.

  1. 1 Thessalonians 4:16–4:17 presence-with-the-Lord promise comparison

    Both anticipate followers being with the coming Lord. John names prepared place and Jesus receiving them to Himself; Paul names resurrection, clouds and meeting Him. The passages are compared without assigning one meaning or chronology to every coming saying in John14.

    Read matching passage

    1 Thessalonians 4:16 For the Lord Himself will descend from heaven with a commanding cry, with an archangel’s voice and with God’s trumpet, and the dead in Christ will rise first.

    1 Thessalonians 4:17 Then we who are alive, who remain, will be caught up together with them in clouds to meet the Lord in the air; and so we will always be with the Lord.

    Pinned target record

John 14:4

And you know the way to where I am going.”

  1. John 14:6 internal way-saying explanation

    The statement that they know the way is clarified by Jesus' I-am-the-way answer following Thomas's objection. The answer concerns coming to the Father through Jesus, not a supplied geographical route to dwelling places.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:6 Jesus says to him, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.

    Pinned target record

John 14:5

Thomas says to Him, “Lord, we do not know where Thou art going. How can we know the way?”

  1. John 13:36 destination-question comparison

    Both disciples ask about Jesus' destination. Thomas links not knowing where He goes with not knowing the way; Peter asks where and receives a cannot-follow-now promise. The disciples, questions and answers remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:36 Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”

    Pinned target record

John 14:6

Jesus says to him, “I am the way and the truth and the life. No one comes to the Father except through Me.

  1. Ephesians 2:18 access-to-the-Father comparison

    Both present access to the Father through Jesus. John names Jesus way, truth and life and says no one comes except through Him; Ephesians names both groups' access through Him in one Spirit. The Spirit and group descriptions are not inserted into John's sentence.

    Read matching passage

    Ephesians 2:18 because through Him we both have access in one Spirit to the Father.

    Pinned target record

John 14:7

If you have known Me, you will also know My Father. From now on you know Him and have seen Him.”

  1. John 8:19 Father-and-Son knowledge comparison

    Both connect knowing Jesus with knowing His Father. John14 adds now knowing and seeing; John8 rebukes questioners who know neither. Their positive promise and negative rebuke remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:19 So they said to Him, “Where is Thy Father?” Jesus answered, “You know neither Me nor My Father. If you knew Me, you would also know My Father.”

John 14:8

Philip says to Him, “Lord, show us the Father, and it is enough for us.”

  1. Exodus 33:18–33:20 divine-vision request comparison

    Both request a divine disclosure. Philip asks Jesus to show the Father; Moses asks the Lord to show His glory and receives a response about glory and the impossibility of seeing His face and living. Revealer, request object and resulting words are not equated.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 33:18 Moses said, “Show me Thy glory.”

    Exodus 33:19 He said, “I will pass before you in My glory and proclaim My name, Lord, before you. I will have mercy on whom I will have mercy, and compassion on whom I will have compassion.”

    Exodus 33:20 He said, “You cannot see My face, for no man shall see My face and live.”

    Pinned target record

John 14:9

Jesus says to him, “Have I been with you for so long a time, and you have not known Me, Philip? The one who has seen Me has seen the Father. How can you say, ‘Show us the Father’?

  1. John 12:45 Father-revelation recurrence

    Both connect seeing Jesus with seeing His Father or Sender. John14 answers named Philip and his request; John12 gives the shorter beholding-the-Sender saying. The statements do not erase the Father/Son distinction.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:45 And the one who beholds Me beholds the One who sent Me.

John 14:10

Do you not believe that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me? The words that I say to you I do not speak from Myself; but the Father, who remains in Me, does His works.

  1. John 12:49 Father-origin speech comparison

    Both deny Jesus speaking from Himself and connect His speech with the Father. John14 adds reciprocal indwelling and the Father's works; John12 describes the Father's command about what to say. Speech, work and command formulations remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:49 Because I have not spoken from Myself, but the Father who sent Me has Himself given Me a command about what I should say and what I should speak.

John 14:11

Believe Me that I am in the Father and the Father is in Me. But if not, believe because of the works themselves.

  1. John 10:37–10:38 works-and-indwelling comparison

    Both appeal to the works as grounds for recognizing Father/Son indwelling even when the speaker is not believed. John10 adds the conditional failure-to-do-Father's-works clause; John14 directly commands belief.

    Read matching passage

    John 10:37 If I do not do My Father’s works, do not believe Me.

    John 10:38 But if I do them, even if you do not believe Me, believe the works, so that you may know and continue to know that the Father is in Me and I in the Father.”

    Pinned target record

John 14:12

Amen, amen, I say to you: the one who believes in Me will also do the works that I do, and will do greater works than these, because I am going to the Father.

  1. Acts 5:12–5:16 promise-and-reported-work comparison

    Acts narrates many signs and healings through apostles after Jesus' ministry, providing concrete followers' works to compare. It does not quantify greater, identify every believer's work, or turn the crowd's hoped-for shadow contact into the stated cause of every healing.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 5:12 Many signs and wonders were taking place among the people through the apostles’ hands. They were all together with one accord in Solomon’s Portico.

    Acts 5:13 None of the rest dared to join them, but the people held them in high esteem.

    Acts 5:14 More and more believers were added to the Lord, multitudes of both men and women,

    Acts 5:15 so that they even carried the sick into the streets and laid them on small beds and mats, so that as Peter came by, at least his shadow might fall upon one of them.

    Acts 5:16 The crowd also came together from the towns around Jerusalem, bringing the sick and those troubled by unclean spirits, and they were all healed.

John 14:13

  1. John 16:23 name-and-request promise comparison

    Both promise response to asking in Jesus' name. John14 says Jesus will do it for the Father's glorification in the Son; John16 explicitly names the Father as giver. Their stated agents and purpose clauses are preserved.

    Read matching passage

    John 16:23 And on that day you will ask Me nothing. Amen, amen, I say to you: if you ask the Father anything, He will give it to you in My name.

John 14:14

If you ask Me anything in My name, I will do it.

  1. John 16:23 request-address comparison

    Both relate requests in Jesus' name to promised response. John14 explicitly says ask Me and I will do it; John16 names asking the Father and His giving. Neither address or agent is silently rewritten to match the other.

    Read matching passage

    John 16:23 And on that day you will ask Me nothing. Amen, amen, I say to you: if you ask the Father anything, He will give it to you in My name.

“If you love Me, you will keep My commandments.

    Read matching passage

    1 John 5:3 For this is the love of God: that we keep His commandments; and His commandments are not burdensome,

John 14:16

  1. John 16:7 Advocate-promise comparison

John 14:17

the Spirit of truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it neither beholds Him nor knows Him. You know Him, because He remains with you and will be in you.

    Both name the Spirit of truth. John14 contrasts the world's inability to receive Him with disciples' knowledge and indwelling promise; John15 names proceeding from the Father, Jesus' sending and witness to Him. The distinct relations and tasks are preserved.

John 14:18

I will not leave you orphans; I am coming to you.

  1. Matthew 28:20 continued-presence promise comparison

    Both assure disciples of Jesus' continuing presence. John says He will not leave them orphans and is coming to them; Matthew says He is with them through the age's consummation. The formulations do not determine an unmentioned date for every Johannine coming promise.

John 14:19

Yet a little while, and the world no longer beholds Me, but you behold Me. Because I live, you also will live.

  1. John 6:57 dependent-life comparison

    Both connect followers' life with Jesus' life. John14 says because I live you will live and contrasts world/disciple sight; John6 connects the living Sender, Jesus and the eater who lives because of Him. The eating image and visibility clauses remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:57 Just as the living Father sent Me and I live because of the Father, so the one who eats Me will also live because of Me.

John 14:20

On that day you will know that I am in My Father, and you in Me, and I in you.

  1. John 17:21–17:23 indwelling-and-unity comparison

    Both passages describe the Father-Son-followers indwelling relation. John14 says Jesus is in the Father and the disciples in Jesus and Jesus in them; John17 additionally says the Father is in Jesus and prays for unity and the world's belief and knowledge. The directions, petitions and predicted recognition remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    John 17:21 so that they may all be one, just as Thou, Father, art in Me and I in Thee, so that they also may be in Us, so that the world may believe that Thou didst send Me.

    John 17:22 And the glory that Thou hast given Me I have given them, so that they may be one just as We are one:

concerning righteousness, because I am going to the Father and you no longer behold Me;

  1. John 16:5 internal departure-premise recurrence

    The going-to-the-Father premise recalls Jesus' earlier statement of going to His Sender in this discourse. The target does not independently explain righteousness or the no-longer-beholding clause; those remain the present verse's stated explanation.

    Read matching passage

    John 16:5 But now I am going to the One who sent Me, and none of you asks Me, ‘Where art Thou going?’

and concerning judgment, because the ruler of this world has been judged.

  1. John 12:31 world-ruler judgment recurrence

    Both relate judgment to the ruler of this world. John16 says he has been judged; John12 says now judgment and future casting out. No new personal identity, date or uniform tense is supplied.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:31 Now is the judgment of this world; now the ruler of this world will be cast out.

  1. Mark 4:33–4:34 teaching-capacity comparison

    Both describe Jesus' teaching in relation to what hearers can receive. John says disciples cannot bear further matters now; Mark speaks to the crowd as able to hear and privately explains to disciples. Audiences, parables and the additional future-teaching context remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    Mark 4:33 With many such parables He spoke the word to them, as they were able to hear.

  1. John 14:26 Spirit-teaching comparison

    Both promise instruction by the coming Spirit. John16 names the Spirit of truth, heard speech and future announcements; John14 names the Father sending in Jesus' name and remembrance of His words. No Sender or speech relation is silently changed.

    The next verse explicitly repeats the Spirit's taking from what is Jesus' and announcing it, explaining it through the Father's possessions being His. This links the stated grounds for the Spirit's glorifying role without making the persons identical.

  1. Luke 22:31–22:32 thematic parallel

    Jesus prays specifically for the disciples given Him; Luke records His prayer that Peter's faith not fail amid Satan's demand to sift the group. Both portray intercession for threatened followers. The group versus Peter focus differs, and this particular prayer's not-for-the-world clause is not made a statement that Jesus never seeks the world's salvation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 22:31 Simon, Simon, behold, Satan demanded to have you all, to sift you like wheat.

    The prayer asks the Father to preserve the disciples and seeks their oneness according to Father and Son; John 10 speaks of preservation from snatching in the Son's and Father's hands and says they are one. John 17:16

They are not from the world, just as I am not from the world.

  1. John 15:19 teaching parallel

    Both passages expressly describe the disciples as not from the world. John 15 explains that Jesus chose them out of the world; the prayer relates their position to His own. This is a repeated belonging-and-allegiance statement, not an assertion that the disciples share every feature of Jesus' heavenly preexistence.

    The prayer calls the Father's word truth; the actual Greek-OT psalm says the beginning of His words is truth and His righteous judgments endure. This is a specific divine-word-and-truth comparison, with sanctifying the disciples as the prayer's additional petition and the exact Psalm numbering retained.

  1. John 20:21 teaching parallel

    The Father-sends-Jesus and Jesus-sends-disciples pattern is explicitly repeated after the resurrection. The prayer speaks of sending them into the world, while the later appearance directly commissions the hearers. The earlier prayer and later risen-Jesus commission retain their order and wording.

John 17:19

and for their sake I sanctify Myself, so that they also may be sanctified in truth.

  1. Hebrews 10:10 thematic parallel

    Jesus dedicates Himself for the disciples' sanctification; Hebrews explains sanctification through the offering of Jesus Christ's body according to God's will. The shared relation is His self-giving for others' sanctification. John frames it in truth, while Hebrews expressly describes the once-for-all bodily offering rather than using identical wording.

    Read matching passage

    Hebrews 10:10 By this will we have been sanctified through the offering of the body of Jesus Christ once for all.

John 17:20

“And I ask not only concerning these, but also concerning those who believe in Me through their word,

  1. Romans 10:14–10:17 thematic parallel

    Jesus extends His prayer to believers reached through the disciples' word; Romans connects believing with hearing and hearing with sent proclamation and Christ's word. Both describe faith mediated through communicated testimony. Romans also says not all obey, so the connection does not turn every hearer into a believer.

    Read matching passage

    Romans 10:14 How, then, are they to call upon One in whom they have not believed? And how are they to believe in One whom they have not heard? And how are they to hear without someone proclaiming?

    Romans 10:15 And how are they to proclaim unless they are sent? Just as it is written, “How beautiful are the feet of those announcing good things!”

    Romans 10:16 But not all obeyed the gospel. For Isaiah says, “Lord, who believed our report?”

    Romans 10:17 So faith is from hearing, and hearing through the word of Christ.

  1. Ephesians 4:3–4:6 thematic parallel

    The prayer seeks believers' oneness in relation to Father and Son, with witness to the world; Ephesians urges unity of the Spirit and names one body, Spirit, hope, Lord, faith, baptism and God and Father. Both ground communal unity in divine relation, with the prayer's mutual-indwelling and mission clauses remaining distinct.

    Ephesians 4:6 one God and Father of all, who is over all and through all and in all.

John 17:22

And the glory that Thou hast given Me I have given them, so that they may be one just as We are one:

  1. 2 Corinthians 3:18 thematic parallel

    Jesus describes giving the disciples received glory for their unity; Paul describes believers beholding the Lord's glory and being transformed from glory to glory through the Spirit. Both concern believers' participation in glory. Giving for unity and transformation into an image are distinct expressions, not interchangeable accounts of one event.

    Read matching passage

    2 Corinthians 3:18 And we all, with unveiled face, beholding as in a mirror the glory of the Lord, are being transformed into the same image from glory to glory, as from the Lord, the Spirit.

    Pinned target record

John 17:23

I in them and Thou in Me, so that they may be made completely one, so that the world may know that Thou didst send Me and didst love them just as Thou didst love Me.

  1. John 14:20–14:21 teaching parallel

    The prayer's I in them and Thou in Me corresponds to the earlier I in My Father, you in Me and I in you, joined with the Father's love for Jesus' followers. The mutual-relation and love motifs recur, while the later prayer additionally gives unity and the world's recognition as purposes.

    Read matching passage

    John 14:20 On that day you will know that I am in My Father, and you in Me, and I in you.

    John 14:21 The one who has My commandments and keeps them is the one who loves Me. And the one who loves Me will be loved by My Father, and I will love him and reveal Myself to him.”

John 17:24

Father, as for what Thou hast given Me, I desire that they too may be with Me where I am, so that they may behold My glory, which Thou hast given Me, because Thou didst love Me before the foundation of the world.

  1. John 14:2–14:3 teaching parallel

    The prayer desires that those given Him be with Him where He is and behold His glory; the earlier promise says He prepares a place and will take the disciples to Himself so they may be where He is. The shared with-Me-where-I-am promise does not supply a new timetable or replace the prayer's before-the-world love clause.

    John 14:2 In My Father’s house are many dwelling places. If it were not so, would I have told you that I am going to prepare a place for you?

    John 14:3 And if I go and prepare a place for you, I am coming again and will take you to Myself, so that where I am, you also may be.

John 17:25

Righteous Father, although the world did not know Thee, I knew Thee, and these knew that Thou didst send Me;

  1. John 8:54–8:55 teaching parallel

    Both passages contrast others' failure to know the Father with Jesus' knowledge of Him. John 8 addresses particular opponents and also speaks of the Father's glorifying Jesus, while the prayer speaks of the world and the disciples' recognition of His sending. Those different audiences and scopes remain visible.

    Read matching passage

    John 8:54 Jesus answered, “If I glorify Myself, My glory is nothing. It is My Father who glorifies Me, of whom you say, ‘He is our God.’

    John 8:55 Yet you have not known Him, but I know Him. And if I say that I do not know Him, I will be a liar like you. But I know Him and keep His word.

John 17:26

and I made Thy name known to them, and will make it known, so that the love with which Thou didst love Me may be in them, and I in them.”

  1. John 15:9–15:10 teaching parallel

    The prayer seeks the Father's love for the Son within the disciples and Jesus in them; the earlier discourse says the Father's love for Jesus is extended through Jesus' love to them and calls them to remain in it. This is the recurring Father-Son-disciples love relation, not another narrated visit or a new set of speakers.

    Read matching passage

    John 15:9 Just as the Father loved Me, I also loved you. Remain in My love.

    John 15:10 If you keep My commandments, you will remain in My love, just as I have kept My Father’s commandments and remain in His love.

  1. Mark 14:32 narrative parallel

    Both accounts move Jesus and the disciples to the place of the ensuing arrest. John specifies a garden beyond the winter-stream Kidron; Mark names Gethsemane and introduces prayer there. The descriptions are compared without replacing John's geographical wording or inserting Mark's place-name into John's actual text.

  1. Luke 22:39 narrative parallel

    Judas knows the place because Jesus often gathered there with the disciples; Luke describes Jesus' customary journey to the Mount of Olives with disciples following. The recurring practice illuminates the familiarity of the setting, without making Luke independently state Judas's knowledge or the garden's precise location.

  1. Mark 14:43 narrative parallel

    Judas arrives with an armed group in both accounts. John specifies a cohort and officers associated with chief priests and Pharisees, plus lights and weapons; Mark names a sword-and-club crowd from chief priests, scribes and elders. John 18:4

  1. John 10:17–10:18 thematic parallel

    Jesus knowingly steps forward to meet the arrest party; His earlier saying explains that He lays down His life of His own accord under the Father's command. The action is compared with the teaching about voluntary self-giving, without making the encounter a denial that others arrest Him or adding a mechanism for His foreknowledge.

    Jesus' I am answer to the requested identity is explicitly recalled by I told you that I am when He asks that the disciples be allowed to leave. This is a repeated self-identification within the arrest encounter, not a claim that the ordinary identity question alone proves a separately reconstructed divine-name quotation.

  1. Psalms 26:2 thematic parallel

    The arrest party goes backward and falls when Jesus speaks; the psalm describes enemies approaching to consume the righteous speaker, growing weak and falling. The concrete approach-and-fall pattern is compared without asserting an explicit Psalm quotation, identical enemies, or a physical mechanism beyond what John narrates.

John 18:7

  1. John 18:4–18:5 narrative parallel

    John explicitly repeats Jesus' question Whom do you seek and the party's Jesus-the-Nazarene answer after the first exchange and the fall. The matched question-and-answer sequence is an internal narrative repetition. It does not exchange the speakers or collapse the repeated stages into a single utterance.

  1. John 10:11–10:12 thematic parallel

    Both accounts identify the high priest's slave's right ear as cut off. John names Peter as the swordsman and Malchus as the slave; Luke leaves them unnamed and expressly narrates Jesus' healing the ear. The shared injury does not add Luke's healing sentence to John or remove John's names.

    Jesus commands the sword returned in both arrest accounts.     John's Jesus accepts the cup the Father gives Him; Mark's earlier prayer asks the Father that the cup be removed while submitting to His will. These are complementary cup-and-Father sayings at different stages, not the same speech or a claim that John records the earlier prayer.

    Jesus says His kingdom does not originate from this world or depend on fighting attendants; earlier He withdraws when people intend to seize Him and make Him king.     Jesus explains before Pilate that He came to bear witness to the truth; the epistle expressly recalls Christ Jesus testifying the good confession before Pontius Pilate.   1. Mark 15:6–15:9 narrative parallel

    Both accounts describe a festival prisoner-release custom and Pilate's proposal to release the King of the Jews. Mark adds the crowd's request and a description of Barabbas's company. This is a comparison of the supplied narrative notices, not external confirmation of the custom's historical scope.

The unused-tomb correspondence does not independently confirm John's garden layout or erase the different architectural descriptions.

John 19:42

  1. Luke 23:53–23:54 narrative parallel

    Both accounts connect Jesus' burial with Preparation and the approaching Sabbath. John expressly gives the tomb's nearness as the reason for using it; Luke states the day's designation and Sabbath's beginning after describing the burial. The shared scheduling context does not invent an extra travel route or harmonized clock.

  1. Mark 16:2–16:4 narrative parallel

    Both accounts place a tomb visit early on the first day of the week and describe the stone already moved. John focuses on Mary Magdalene while it is still dark; Mark's preceding list gives several women and says the sun has risen. Group and light descriptions are retained without inventing separate departure and arrival times to reconcile them.

    Matthew 14:19 He ordered the crowds to recline on the grass. Taking the five loaves and the two fish, He looked up to heaven, blessed, broke the loaves, and gave them to the disciples, and the disciples to the crowds.

John 21:11

So Simon Peter went up and drew the net onto the land, full of large fish, a hundred and fifty-three; and though there were so many, the net was not torn.

  1. Luke 5:6–5:7 narrative parallel

    Both narratives describe a great fish catch and its strain on the equipment. Luke says the nets begin to tear and two boats become loaded; John gives 153 large fish and expressly says the net is not torn. Number, net outcome and boat details are retained without an invented symbolic fish count or an identical catch.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 5:6 When they did this, they enclosed a great multitude of fish, and their nets began to tear.

    Luke 5:7 They signaled to their partners in the other boat to come and help them. They came and filled both boats, so that they began to sink.

John 21:12

Jesus says to them, “Come, have breakfast.” But none of the disciples dared to question Him, “Who art Thou?” knowing that it was the Lord.

  1. Luke 24:41–24:43 narrative parallel

    Both risen-Jesus scenes involve food with the disciples, but the roles and recognition differ. John gives His breakfast invitation to disciples who know the Lord; Luke gives His request and explicit fish-eating while the group still struggles to believe for joy. Luke's eating statement is not added as an explicit action in John's invitation.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 24:41 While they still did not believe for joy and were marveling, He said to them, “Do you have anything to eat here?”

    Luke 24:42 They gave Him a portion of broiled fish.

    Luke 24:43 And He took it and ate before them.

John 21:13

Jesus comes and takes the bread and gives it to them, and the fish likewise.

  1. John 6:11 narrative parallel

    Jesus takes and distributes bread and fish in both scenes. The earlier sign feeds the reclining crowd with thanksgiving; the shore scene gives the same foods to the disciples after the catch. The recipients and scale differ, and the earlier thanksgiving is not inserted into a scene that does not explicitly repeat it.

    Read matching passage

    John 6:11 Then Jesus took the loaves, gave thanks, and distributed them to those reclining, and likewise from the fish, as much as they wanted.

John 21:14

This was now the third time Jesus was revealed to the disciples after being raised from the dead.

  1. John 20:19–20:29 narrative parallel

    The third-revelation notice follows the two narrated group appearances: the first-day evening without Thomas and the gathering after eight days with him. This relates John's count to those prior scenes without making it a universal count of every appearance or denying Mary's earlier encounter and other supplied Gospel accounts.

    Read matching passage

    John 20:19 Then, when it was evening on that day, the first day of the week, and the doors were shut where the disciples were, because of fear of the Jews, Jesus came and stood in the midst, and says to them, “Peace to you.”

    John 20:20 And having said this, He showed them His hands and His side. Then the disciples rejoiced when they saw the Lord.

    John 20:21 Then Jesus said to them again, “Peace to you. Just as the Father has sent Me, I also send you.”

    John 20:22 And having said this, He breathed and says to them, “Receive the Holy Spirit.

    John 20:23 If you forgive the sins of any, they have been forgiven them; if you retain those of any, they have been retained.”

    John 20:24 But Thomas, one of the Twelve, who was called Didymus, was not with them when Jesus came.

    John 20:25 So the other disciples were saying to him, “We have seen the Lord.” But he said to them, “Unless I see in His hands the mark of the nails and put my finger into the mark of the nails and put my hand into His side, I certainly will not believe.”

    John 20:26 And after eight days His disciples were inside again, and Thomas with them. Jesus comes, the doors being shut, and stood in the midst and said, “Peace to you.”

    John 20:27 Then He says to Thomas, “Bring your finger here and see My hands, and bring your hand and put it into My side; and do not become unbelieving, but believing.”

    John 20:28 Thomas answered and said to Him, “My Lord and my God!”

    John 20:29 Jesus says to him, “Because you have seen Me, have you believed? Blessed are those who have not seen and have believed.”

John 21:15

So when they had eaten breakfast, Jesus says to Simon Peter, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me more than these?” He says to Him, “Yes, Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee.” He says to him, “Feed My lambs.”

  1. 1 Peter 5:1–5:4 thematic parallel

    Jesus charges Peter to feed His lambs; the epistle's fellow-elder exhortation calls for willing care of God's flock under the Chief Shepherd. This is a concrete pastoral-care comparison, not identical wording or a new determination of the ambiguous more than these in Jesus' love question.

    Read matching passage

    1 Peter 5:1 I exhort the elders among you, as a fellow elder and a witness of the sufferings of Christ, and also a participant in the glory about to be revealed:

    1 Peter 5:2 Shepherd the flock of God among you, exercising oversight, not under compulsion but willingly, according to God; not for shameful gain but eagerly;

    1 Peter 5:3 not as lording it over those allotted to you, but becoming examples to the flock.

    1 Peter 5:4 And when the Chief Shepherd is manifested, you will receive the unfading crown of glory.

John 21:16

He says to him again a second time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” He says to Him, “Yes, Lord, Thou knowest that I love Thee.” He says to him, “Shepherd My sheep.”

  1. Acts 20:28 thematic parallel

    Jesus tells Peter to shepherd His sheep; the elders' charge in Acts tells Spirit-appointed overseers to shepherd the Church of God. Both entrust care of God's people through the shepherd-and-flock image. Individual and plural recipients and the stated appointing roles remain distinct rather than an identical commissioning scene.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 20:28 Pay attention to yourselves and to all the flock, among whom the Holy Spirit has placed you as overseers, to shepherd the Church of God, which He acquired through His own blood.

John 21:17

He says to him the third time, “Simon, son of John, do you love Me?” Peter was grieved because He said to him the third time, “Do you love Me?” And he said to Him, “Lord, Thou knowest all things; Thou knowest that I love Thee.” Jesus says to him, “Feed My sheep.

  1. John 13:37–13:38 narrative parallel

    The explicitly third love question and renewed feeding charge are compared with Peter's earlier life-pledge and Jesus' prediction of three denials. The triadic faithfulness-and-failure pattern is concrete in John's narrative. It does not make the three questions a newly supplied one-for-one absolution formula or introduce love-word distinctions absent from this English text.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:37 Peter said to Him, “Lord, why can I not follow Thee now? I will lay down my life for Thee.”

    John 13:38 Jesus answered, “Will you lay down your life for Me? Amen, amen, I tell you: a rooster will certainly not crow until you deny Me three times.”

John 21:18

Amen, amen, I say to you: when you were younger, you tied your belt around yourself and walked where you wished; but when you grow old, you will stretch out your hands, and another will tie your belt around you and carry you where you do not wish.”

  1. 2 Peter 1:13–1:14 thematic parallel

    John narrates Jesus' forecast of Peter's belt being tied by another and his being carried where he does not wish, interpreted next as death; the epistle says the Lord made clear the approaching putting-off of the speaker's tent. Both concern a Christ-disclosed death prospect, without adding a date, an inverted crucifixion, or proof that the epistle quotes this exact disclosure.

    Read matching passage

    2 Peter 1:13 And I consider it right, as long as I am in this tent, to stir you up by reminding you,

    2 Peter 1:14 knowing that I will soon put off my tent, as our Lord Jesus Christ also made clear to me.

    Pinned target record

John 21:19

Now He said this to indicate by what kind of death he would glorify God. And having said this, He says to him, “Follow Me.”

  1. John 12:32–12:33 narrative parallel

    Both narrator explanations interpret Jesus' preceding words as indicating the kind of death: John 12 concerns Jesus' being lifted up, and John 21 concerns Peter's forecast. The recurring explanatory formula does not exchange the persons or establish an identical execution method from the two images alone.

    Read matching passage

    John 12:32 And I, if I am lifted up from the earth, will draw all people to Myself.”

    John 12:33 He said this to signify by what kind of death He was about to die.

    Pinned target record

  2. John 13:36 narrative parallel

    Jesus earlier tells Peter he cannot follow now but will follow later; after the death interpretation He now directly commands Follow Me. This is a specific Petrine following promise and renewed summons, not a new calendar date or an assertion that every earlier inability has been explained in the same way.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:36 Simon Peter said to Him, “Lord, where art Thou going?” Jesus answered him, “Where I go, you cannot follow Me now, but you will follow later.”

John 21:20

Turning, Peter sees the disciple whom Jesus loved following, the one who also leaned back at the supper upon His chest and said, “Lord, who is the one handing Thee over?”

  1. John 13:23–13:25 narrative parallel

    John expressly recalls the disciple who reclined against Jesus at supper and asked about the hander-over. The earlier supper narrative records the beloved disciple's position, Peter's signal and the question. The retrospective formulation is fuller than the earlier Lord, who is it, and neither passage supplies the disciple's personal name.

    Read matching passage

    John 13:23 One of His disciples, whom Jesus loved, was reclining in Jesus’ bosom.

    John 13:24 So Simon Peter signaled to this one to ask whom He was speaking about.

    John 13:25 Leaning back thus upon Jesus’ chest, he said to Him, “Lord, who is it?”

John 21:21

So Peter, seeing him, says to Jesus, “Lord, and what about this man?”

  1. Acts 1:6–1:8 narrative parallel

    In both post-resurrection exchanges, a question about the future is answered with a boundary on the disciples' concern and a task from Jesus. Peter asks about the other disciple before being told to follow; the group asks about kingdom timing before being commissioned as witnesses. Neither question is assigned an unspoken motive or its requested timetable supplied.

    Read matching passage

    Acts 1:6 So those who had come together asked Him, “Lord, is this the time when Thou art restoring the kingdom to Israel?”

    Acts 1:7 He said to them, “It is not for you to know times or seasons that the Father has set by His own authority.

    Acts 1:8 But you will receive power when the Holy Spirit comes upon you, and you will be My witnesses in Jerusalem and [in] all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.”

John 21:22

Jesus says to him, “If I wish him to remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow Me.”

  1. John 21:19 narrative parallel

    Jesus repeats the personal Follow Me charge after Peter asks about the other disciple. The earlier command follows Peter's death forecast; the later one contrasts Peter's task with a conditional statement about the other's remaining. The refrain does not turn that conditional into a promise of immortality.

    Read matching passage

    John 21:19 Now He said this to indicate by what kind of death he would glorify God. And having said this, He says to him, “Follow Me.”

John 21:23

So this word went out among the brothers, that this disciple does not die. But Jesus did not say to him that he does not die, but, “If I wish him to remain until I come, what is that to you?”

  1. John 21:22 narrative parallel

    The narrator explicitly corrects the undying-disciple rumor by repeating Jesus' actual conditional words from the preceding answer. This is a direct quotation-and-correction relationship. The if is retained, with no claim that Jesus predicted the disciple would never die or that the rumor establishes his later biography.

    Read matching passage

    John 21:22 Jesus says to him, “If I wish him to remain until I come, what is that to you? You follow Me.”

John 21:24

This is the disciple who bears witness about these things and who wrote these things; and we know that his testimony is true.

  1. John 19:35 narrative parallel

    Both narrative witness statements affirm the testimony's truth. John 19 describes the one who saw and bears witness so others may believe; John 21 adds writing and the plural we know. The recurring formula is compared without assigning an unrecorded personal name or settling a new editorial theory about the plural voice.

    Read matching passage

    John 19:35 And the one who has seen has borne witness, and his testimony is true; and he knows that he speaks the truth, so that you also may believe.

    Pinned target record

John 21:25

And there are also many other things that Jesus did, which, if each one were written, I suppose that even the world itself would not contain the books being written.

  1. John 20:30 narrative parallel

    Both ending statements distinguish Jesus' wider activity from what the book records. John 20 specifically mentions signs before the disciples; John 21 broadens to other things and the world-and-books expression. The correspondence does not literalize the capacity rhetoric or establish particular unwritten deeds as verified facts.

    Read matching passage

    John 20:30 Now Jesus also did many other signs before His disciples, which are not written in this book;

Reviewed units still unfinished

  • John 2:2: The invitation is to the wedding already set in Cana; the surrounding mother and wine-shortage notices do not identify the hosts or explain who issued the invitation. No generic wedding comparison is retained.
  • John 7:53: Each going home is kept within the original double-bracketed passage. Jesus going to the Mount of Olives is a distinct destination, not a basis for mapping every persons home or claiming independent attestation of the departure.
  • John 18:34: The actual ensuing reply names Jesus nation and chief priests as the delivering party. It does not explicitly choose the from-yourself or others-told-you alternative; no unspoken source or motive for Pilates question is inserted.
